=== Designing Systems that Scale ===


--- Scalability: Introduction ---

1
(jolly synth music)
2
One of the biggest challenges
3
faced by engineers in Big Tech is scalability.
4
It's one thing to come up with a clever algorithm or system,
5
but yet another to build it in such a way
6
that it can handle thousands of transactions per second,
7
and petabytes of data.
8
Your ability to design systems at scale
9
is one of the main things
10
your interviewer will be looking for.
11
Fortunately, the concept of horizontal partitioning
12
solves a lot of problems,
13
but it takes looking at a few examples
14
to understand how to structure your systems in such a way
15
that they can be parallelized in this manner.
16
In this first section,
17
we'll talk about various ways to scale systems
18
to massive amounts of traffic
19
working our way up to systems where the sky is the limit.

So let's talk about one way of scaling up further.
132
Let's say that I do want to take more traffic
133

Again, we start with our clients

So vertical scaling
139

For a long time with databases,

And these things got really, really expensive,
150

Same thing applies to your web server here.
152
So I have a big, fat host here running HTTPD presumably,
153

So to some extent,

So if I have a little personal website that, again,

But again, not something

You can only get so big of a computer

There's only so many CPUs you can get in a system,
177
there's only so much memory.

At some point, you're gonna hit a wall.

So vertical scaling only gets you so far,

but you need to know what it is.

Similarly, I can make the database server bigger too.

We talked about that a little bit

Same idea.

Maybe I need more storage.

To a point, I can just throw bigger and bigger

to an extent, just that way.

The only good thing about this

So in this system,
193

196

Maybe that's okay, probably isn't though.

So again, just to summarize there,
205

That is the problem.
207
So you still have single points of failure here.
208
The only upside of this
209
is that you have fewer servers to maintain.
210
Now, horizontal scaling.
211
This is kind of where it's at.
212
So it's a pretty safe bet
213
that for most modern system design problems,
214
where you're being asked to develop a system at large scale,
215
they want you to take a more horizontal scaling approach.
216
So again, you have your clients out there in the world
217
hitting you through the internet.
218
The idea here is that instead of a single server,
219
you have multiple servers
220
and you have some sort of a load balancer
221
between the internet and those servers
222
that are distributing that load in some fair, even manner.
223
We could talk about load balancers strategies.
224
There's round robin, there's partitioning it,
225
there's load bouncers that can actually
226
look at the available capacity on a server
227
and try to route traffic intelligently that way.
228
So that's a whole other can of worms.
229
But the idea is that there is some,
230
either device or software process running somewhere
231
that is distributing that load coming in from the internet
232
to a whole fleet of servers.
233
So the beauty of that is that if one of those goes down,
234
assuming I have enough excess capacity provisioned,
235
the user doesn't need to know.
236
The load bouncer can realize that a host is down
237
and reroute the traffic around it automatically
238
so that you have no downtime.
239
Pretty cool, right?
240
Maybe they all still point to a single database.
241
We could scale that out as well, if we wanted to,
242
but in this case,
243
maybe our database isn't really a choke point
244
and we have good redundancy on it somehow.
245
We'll get more to database scaling later on,
246
but at least in this architecture,
247
our front-end web servers are gonna keep on serving, right?
248
Maybe they have some sort of a cache
249
in front of the database,
250
so they can still do something meaningful
251
if that database were to go down.
252
But the idea of horizontal scaling
253
is that as I get more and more traffic,
254
I just add more and more servers to the fleet
255
and the load balancer will distribute that traffic
256
amongst those servers.
257
So you can see, you can pretty much scale infinitely
258
with this idea, right?
259
If I have millions of transactions coming in,
260
throw in enough servers in there and you'll be good.
261
There are some finer points about,
262
where these are located geographically
263
and what servers, what data centers.
264
We'll get into that.
265
But this is the basic idea of horizontal scaling.
266
It allows an infinite,
267
well, practically infinite way of scaling things up,
268
where the more servers you can throw in there,
269
the more traffic you can handle.
270
There's really no bound to it in this case.
271
The downside, of course,
272
is that there's more stuff to maintain.
273
So let's talk about stateful and stateless stuff here.
274
So the catch here is that this really only works well
275
if your web servers are what we call stateless.
276
What I mean by that is that subsequent requests
277
should not depend on something being stored on that server
278
from a previous request, okay?
279
Now, you can store stuff in the database.
280
That's fine.
281
But the servers themselves,
282
those web servers need to be stateless
283
because I don't know
284
where that previous request got routed to.
285
So I cannot assume on any given server
286
that I have any information about the previous hits
287
from that given user, right?
288
So a stateless web server means that any web server
289
can handle any request at any time, okay?
290
Again, it's okay to use the database
291
to tie those together and have sort of a history,
292
but any individual server cannot assume that that server
293
is the one that actually served
294
previous requests to a given user.
295
That's what I mean by stateless.
296
It just means that we're baking in the assumption
297
that any request for a user could have gone to any server.
298
And we cannot assume that our server
299
knows anything about those previous requests directly.
300
Okay?
301
So how do you go about choosing one of those architectures?
302
Well, in general, a theme in this course
303
that I'm gonna be preaching is simplicity.
304
You should always choose the simplest architecture
305
that meets your projected requirements,
306
but no simpler than that.
307
So, yeah, I mean, if you're being asked
308
to develop something on the internet
309
where it's just like a little phone tool, maybe,
310
for one team, there's probably not a whole lot of point
311
in over-engineering that
312
and building some huge horizontally scalable system, right?
313
Maybe a simpler one is okay.
314
Maybe vertical scaling is okay in that instance.
315
But the truth is, for most of these system design interviews
316
at the big companies, that's not the sort of thing
317
they're gonna ask you to design.
318
They're gonna ask you to design YouTube
319
or design Google, design some massive system
320
where you need to have that scalability.
321
So usually, it's a safe bet that you want to go
322
with a more horizontally scaled,
323
horizontally partitioned architecture there instead.
324
So let's dive into that in more depth
325
in our next section here, but big takeaway here,
326
vertical scaling just means that you're throwing
327
bigger machines at the problem.
328
Horizontal scaling means you're throwing
329
more machines at the problem and distributing the load
330
throughout those different machines, okay?
331
That's the main thing to take away from this lecture.


=== Working with Big Data ===


--- Failover Strategies ---

-: So a little side note here:
where do servers come from?
You know, we've talked about
these horizontally scaled systems
where we just keep throwing more
and more servers at them,
and that's real easy to do on paper.
But somewhere these servers physically exist,
and they need to have power and networking,
and they need to be cooled down,
and all that stuff.
They need to be in a secure location.
So where are these things, right?
Well, sometimes they're provisioned
in your own company's data centers,
and if you are working
at a massive company, like Amazon or Google,
they probably have their own data centers
that they're running their own cloud services out of, right?
So that's where these things are gonna be.
And you'll have to work with whoever manages
those data centers on the operation side
to provision the servers that you need.
A lot of times that's easier said than done
because, you know, companies have finite budgets,
and finite amount of space for servers,
and pretty often they run out.
So sometimes that's half the battle,
just getting the servers that you need
for a system in the first place,
who's gonna pay for them, right?
It's gotta come outta somebody's budget.
Alternatively, you might be using cloud services out there.
So, you know, if you're not one
of the big cloud service providers
and working for Amazon or working for Google,
maybe you're still using their servers
and just paying for them as you need them.
So as an individual, I rent time on Amazon's cloud,
Amazon Web Services,
and they have a product called EC2,
which is Elastic Compute Cloud.
That allows me to rent time on virtual machines as needed.
So I can actually rent a piece of a machine
for as long or as little bit of time as I need, right?
So that's pretty cool from my standpoint.
I can really control my own costs,
and I can provision those servers
whenever I want to at a drop of a hat.
So, you know, for reasonable loads,
these cloud services generally have enough excess capacity
sitting around ready to go,
that you're not gonna have to think
about the provisioning too much
or think about where these servers are being taken care of
because the cloud service provider
will be handling all the maintenance for you.
You're still gonna have to deal with the case
where one of these servers goes down.
They will still go down,
and you will still need to have
an architecture in place to deal with that.
So from a system design standpoint,
doesn't matter a whole lot where these servers are.
You're still gonna be facing the same problems.
One good thing about these cloud services, though,
and this also applies to Google Compute Engine
or Azure VMs, whatever you might be using,
whatever your favorite provider is,
they actually do allow you
to provision these things in different regions,
in different data centers.
They might call them availability zones.
And this allows you to have more control
about your redundancy.
We'll talk about that more later on,
how to design systems that can actually survive
an entire continent going up in flames, God forbid.
Also, there are some services out there
on these web services
that don't force you to think about servers at all.
These are called serverless servers.
Some examples would be Amazon's Lambda service
where you just give it a little snippet of code to run,
and they figure out where to run it,
or Amazon Kinesis, which handles, you know,
streaming data around from one point to another.
Amazon Athena, that's kind of their data warehouse product
that allows you to query a data warehouse or a data lake.
These are all services where the actual provisioning
of the servers themselves are hidden from you.
So they bill you by, you know, the transaction,
how much you're using the service.
You don't actually see
or have any control over the actual servers
that are running these servers yourself.
So they provide sort of an extra layer of abstraction
where you don't even have to think about
the system architecture from a hardware standpoint.
So sometimes that has its place, right?
We'll talk about that more later on as well.
So let's talk more about the database side of things
and how you scale that.
We talked earlier about scaling out your web servers,
but your database is also a potential
single point of failure.
So there are ways of horizontally scaling that too.
There's a few ways of doing it.
So one is called a cold standby server,
and this is just saying, all right,
I got my clients going through the web,
talking to my web server,
and I got this big old single point
of failure database here, right?
What happens when that database goes down?
Well, one approach would be to just have a periodic backup
that runs of that database host
where I'm just dumping off a backup
to some storage somewhere.
Maybe it's in S3.
Maybe it's some other server somewhere.
Maybe it's a tape.
People still use those.
But the idea is that if that host goes down,
I have another one that's on standby
that's ready to take its place,
but it's not quite ready, right?
Basically, I just make sure that I have the ability
to get my hands
on a replacement database host really quickly,
but I need to restore that backup first.
So when that host goes down,
I'm gonna restore that backup file
to my standby, my cold standby database host,
and then I'll redirect the traffic to that new host,
and things should be back up and running.
A few downsides to this, right?
So first of all, that does increase
the amount of downtime that I have here.
I can't actually get the system back up
and running until I've provisioned that new server
and I've restored it backup to it.
That's a lot of data.
That can take a really long time.
You know, I've seen that take days on some Oracle hosts,
and furthermore, any data that happened
after that last backup will be lost.
So this is better than nothing,
but not much better than nothing. (laughs)
You know, this is also a case where you need to be able
to tolerate a decent amount of downtime.
You need to be able to tolerate some data loss,
and the only upsides are that is cheap, right?
So I just need to have access to that server when I need it.
Beyond that, it's cheap, it's simple,
but for a real production, customer facing feature,
probably not a good choice.
However, again, if it's some internal tool
where it's okay to be down for a little while,
and it's okay to lose a few hours of data,
eh, maybe it makes sense,
but that's what cold standby is.
Again, probably not
what the interviewer's gonna be looking for
for a massively scalable system.
The next step up is what we call a warm standby.
So, again, we have our clients out there talking to us
through the internet, talking to a web server,
and talking to a big single point of failure database.
So in this a little bit different.
We have another database sitting out here,
but it's always ready to go.
So, basically, we have something called replication.
That is constantly copying data
from the primary database to a backup database host.
So instead of having a periodic backup
that I then need to manually restore,
I'm making sure that this second database host
is always warm.
It's always got a copy of the data there,
just sitting there ready to go, right?
So there are many ways of handling replication.
One would be to actually write the data simultaneously
to each server,
but most databases
have their own replication mechanisms built in
where if you have more than one database host,
you can just turn on replication,
and the database will manage that for you automatically.
So as things are written, they will be copied off
to the other backup hosts automatically.
Again, there might be a tiny window
where you might lose data,
but compared to just having a periodic backup,
this is a far superior approach.
So now, if that primary database goes down, what happens?
We just redirect traffic to our warm standby.
And since it's already sitting there with a replicated copy
of all that data, nobody really knows what happened, right?
There'll be a really quick blip of outage there
as your router there decides
that the primary database is down
and somebody has to switch it over.
Hopefully, that happened automatically.
But from the user standpoint,
the downtime's very minimal.
Very little if any data was lost.
So this is definitely a better way to go.
Still fairly simple.
You know, replication, like I said,
is usually just a switch you turn on in a lot of databases,
so it's not a whole lot of work you have to manage.
However, it doesn't mean you're gonna have increased load
on that backup database,
so that makes that more likely to fail.
But still, we only have a couple of servers here
to take care of,
so it's still a pretty simple solution.
However, we're still basically doing
vertical scaling here, right?
We still just have one giant database host.
We just have another giant database host sitting there ready
to take its place if need be.
So still we're limited by
how much traffic a single host can provide.
Taking things a little bit further,
we can talk about a hot standby situation as well.
So, again, we have our client applications
talking through the internet or the network somehow
to your application server.
And from there we talk to our primary database node.
Now, in this case, we are having
another standby database here,
but it is being replicated again in real time.
So the difference here between warm and hot
is that we are actually doing
that replication in real time.
There is no delay.
It is always live.
And as a result of that,
we can actually use that standby node
as another read source.
So we can actually read simultaneously from both
of these instances if we need to.
So what happens if that connection goes down to the primary?
Well, in that case, we just rely on the fact that
that backup has already been updated, and it's live,
and it's ready to go at all times,
and we can just start redirecting writes there
as well in addition to reads.
And that is a hot standby.
Taking it even further,
we have the concept of multi-primary,
which is getting us even closer
to true horizontal scaling.
Again, our clients talk
through the internet to some app server.
You have a primary database,
but you basically have more than one of them.
So we are actually writing simultaneously
from the application to both of these servers now.
We're not relying on replication to do that.
So this is what we call a multi-primary setup
where we can actually read and write
from both of those nodes at any time.
And if one goes down, that's A-okay.
We just take it outta service
and just start hitting that backup all the time instead.
Now, of course, recovery is its own problem.
Somehow we need to get that new replacement database
for the one that died back up to sync
before we can put it back in service,
but not gonna go there yet.
So yeah, we're getting closer to true horizontal scaling.
Let's talk about that a little bit more.


=== Designing Generative AI Systems ===


--- Sharding Databases / NoSQL ---

1
So at a very high level, this is what a modern,
2
scalable database design would look like.
3
So you want to remember this one.
4
Basically at the top, we have some sort of a router
5
that's routing requests from your clients to the database.
6
So this is saying, okay, I've got a request
7
to write this data out to this table here.
8
Where do I send it?
9
So it's gonna to pick one of many shards here.
10
A shard is just a horizontal partition of your database.
11
So we can have up to N shards
12
here in this diagram, we have shard one,
13
shard two, shard three,
14
and we can keep adding these horizontally as need be
15
as we need more and more capacity
16
or more and more redundancy.
17
And each shard might have
18
one or more backups associated with it.
19
So kind of a, you know, the best of both worlds here,
20
we have like the idea of a hot standby there
21
with these shards in a sense,
22
'cause they might be partitioned, right?
23
So that router might be saying,
24
I want to send one subset of the data to this shard
25
and another subset to this other shard
26
or maybe to multiple shards
27
to have a backup of the backups okay.
28
But each shard might have its own backup host
29
that's running replication basically in the background
30
so that if that shard goes down,
31
its backup hosts can take its place right away.
32
So what's great about sharding is that it lets us
33
to route a given request to a given partition.
34
So again, I can horizontally partition this database
35
to my heart's content,
36
maybe store a little different segments of the data
37
on different shards, through a hash function of some sort.
38
And each shard has its own backup mechanism as well.
39
So that I have not only scalability,
40
but also resiliency because if any one shard host goes down,
41
there's another one waiting to take its place.
42
And hopefully that backup will stay up long enough
43
for me to get a new backup up and running.
44
So at a high level, that sharding, that's kind of
45
the fundamental idea behind a lot of modern,
46
scalable database technologies.
47
Now, one thing you might note here is that
48
because I'm partitioning the data this way,
49
combining data across shards
50
gets to be a little bit hairy, right?
51
So if I need to issue a request that actually joins data
52
on shard one with data from shard three,
53
how does that happen?
54
You know, the, the client has to do that somehow.
55
So that gets a little bit sticky.
56
Because of that, we typically want to design things
57
such that we minimize joins or complex SQL operations,
58
and try to think about how to structure the data
59
in such a way that it's a simple
60
key value look up as much as possible.
61
So if I can hash that key to a given shard,
62
you know, say, I want to write some information
63
for customer number 123,
64
I might be able to hash number 123 to shard number two.
65
And I know shard number two will have all the data
66
for customer number 123,
67
and I can read and write all the data for that customer
68
to my heart's content within that shard.
69
So if you think about how to organize your data
70
into key value data stores,
71
that makes it really easy to apply this idea of sharding
72
and allow you to scale up your database
73
to your heart's content.

Now, another example is Cassandra.

Now Cassandra gets around that whole problem

187
So remember in MongoDB,

we had three identical configuration servers

Well, Cassandra has an idea to solve that problem.
201
That's a little bit different.

So Cassandra has this ring system that you see here
203
where your data is split up into all these different nodes.
204
So it's still sharded,
205
and again, a note is just another way
206
of talking about a shard.
207
Now, a particular shard, a particular segment
208
of your data will probably be replicated
209
on multiple notes for redundancy purposes.
210
So it's not the case that a single server holds
211
the only copy of a set of your data
212
that will be replicated
213
to other nodes in some intelligent fashion.
214
But the point here is at any one of these nodes
215

216

218
So there is no single point of failure
219

220

221
Now there is a trade-off here because any node
222

223
amongst all of these nodes in order to stay up to date.
224
So all of these nodes need to know
225
what's where at all times,
226
and that means that as a trade-off for this architecture,
227
we have what's called eventual consistency
228
because it takes time
229
for that data to propagate throughout this ring,
230
through every note in your system.
231
So what we're doing here is basically giving up

that single point of failure problem in a different way.
233
We're getting a little bit more availability
234
in that we don't have a single primary server here
235
for the system at any one time,

which means that as data is written,

239

240
that you just wrote.
241
Maybe you wrote the data to one node,
242

243

244
So that's the trade off there.
245
But if you're in a situation where a small delay
246
between writing some data and reading it back is okay,

maybe an architecture like Cassandra
248
could work out well for you.

So we can't really talk about a NoSQL databases

So let's imagine that we have a system

just a really simple data structure here.

So at the end, you know,

So if I needed to actually display this to the end user,
381

I need to actually tie that to some customer name.
383
So there might be a separate customer table,
384

386
whatever it is, to be able to communicate
387

388
what the status of their reservation is and whatnot, right?
389
So this is an example of a normalized data set,
390
where we have the data broken up into two separate tables,
391
and that's done to minimize the use of space, right?
392
So instead of duplicating the names and phone numbers
393
on every single reservation
394
in the reservation table,
395
instead, I refer to this customer's table
396
through the customer ID,
397
you know, in database terms,
398
that's called the primary key, right?
399
So what I'm doing here is called a join
400
where I'm joining the customer table
401
with the reservation table to output the final output,
402
where I have a reservation ID.
403
That includes the customer's name, phone number,
404
whatever else I want to show.
405
So historically this is how you would do it.
406
You know, this is what you do in a relational database,
407
it's what makes a relational database relational.
408
You can have these relations between tables
409
that are tied together this way
410
and that's called normalized data.
411
The advantages of this are that there are less storage space
412
and an even bigger advantage is that
413
if you need to update a customer's information,
414
you can do that in one place, right?
415
So by having this separate customer table,
416
if a customer changes their name or phone number
417
or email address, whatever it is,
418
I can do that in one place in the customer ID table.
419
And that will be reflected everywhere else automatically,
420
because it's all just one place where that's stored.
421
Now, it does mean that I need
422
to potentially do more lookups though.
423
So if I were storing this
424
in a horizontally distributed, NoSQL database, right?
425
I'd have to actually do two different hits to do this.
426
If I wanted to retrieve this information,
427
I would first need to retrieve the reservation table
428
and then say, okay, I want information for customer ID.
429
I'm going to do a second query
430
to get back the customer information
431
associated with that customer ID.
432
So in a traditional relational database,
433
you would do this with one SQL query
434
that ties them together through a joint operation.
435
And on NOSQL databases,
436
you can probably do the same thing
437
but under the hood, it would have to actually do two queries
438
to fulfill that request one to the reservation ID table
439
and another one to the customer ID table.
440
Practically speaking, the relational database
441
would have to do the same thing, right?
442
So it's not really all that different.
443
Now, in contrast, if I want to do
444
a denormalized representation,
445
it would look like this,
446
where I would just have
447
that information duplicated all the time.
448
So instead of having a separate customer ID table,
449
I'm actually going to duplicate the customer name
450
and phone number in each, in every reservation ID row there.
451
So this is what we call denormalized data.
452
Why would I do that?
453
Well, it's more efficient, right?
454
So if I really, really care about performance
455
and scaling more than anything,
456
this allows me to get with a single database hit
457
everything I need to fulfill a request
458
for displaying a reservation, right?
459
So if I just want to say, give me all the information
460
about reservation ID number one,
461
that one hit to the database
462
will give me back everything I need,
463
including the customer name and phone number and whatnot.
464
So the advantage of denormalized data
465
is that it lets you get everything back
466
in one database query.
467
However, there are downsides, right?
468
The big downside is that updates are hard.
469
If I'm gonna change a customer's phone number,
470
I would have to go through every single row
471
of that reservation table, looking for that customer ID
472
and changing that phone number every single time
473
that's a lot of potential for things to get out of sync
474
and for things to go wrong, right?
475
And it's also a pain,
476
like that's a hard thing to do atomically, right?
477
So it means that any changes to the customer information
478
is probably gonna be eventually consistent.
479
It's also gonna be wasteful in terms of space.
480
I'm duplicating this big string of customer names
481
and a big string of phone numbers,
482
everything else on every single row
483
and I didn't really have to do that right?
484
With a normalized representation.
485
That's all stored in one place.
486
So typically people talk about denormalized data
487
in the context of these NoSQL databases.
488
And they say, yeah, if you're gonna be doing,
489
you know, massive scale,
490
and then you just want to do one database instead of two
491
or three or whatever it might be
492
denormalization is the way to go.
493
But I say, that's not always the case, right?
494
It depends, so when this comes up in an interview setting,
495
I think it's good to demonstrate a knowledge
496
of the trade-offs between these two,
497
but it's not always a clear cut choice
498
between these two representations.
499
I mean, generally the way I would approach it
500
would be starting from a normalized approach
501
because that is gonna be the simpler approach
502
for handling updates going forward.
503
And it's more space efficient as well.
504
So those are both big advantages.
505
I mean, I think the right answer here,
506
if people are asking you whether to choose
507
between normalized and denormalized
508
is to think about the customer experience,
509
what are the actual calls that you're gonna be making
510
to the database for the most common operations?
511
And do you expect that to be a bottleneck
512
if that involves more than one database hit?
513
If the answer is no, or I don't know yet,
514
then I would say you want
515
to start with the normalized data structure
516
and you can always go to denormalized later
517
if you find that you do have a performance bottleneck
518
in doing those databases to construct those queries,
519
you know, if you do need to cut your traffic
520
to the database in half,
521
then denormalizing your data is a good way to do it
522
if you're a joint operation just had two tables, right?
523
So, you know, I think it's a good trick
524
that you should talk about
525
and make sure people know that,
526
you know, the denormalization trick,
527
but it shouldn't be your go-to, I don't think.
528
You know, you should be cognizant of the actual queries
529
your application will be issuing
530
the kind of scale you're gonna be doing that at.
531
And sometimes that will warrant normalizing your data
532
sometimes it won't.
533
But you know, you need
534
to just demonstrate to the interviewer
535
that you know, that what those trade-offs are,
536
and you can make the right decision
537
based on what the traffic and performance bottlenecks
538
actually turned out to be in your application.


=== Design Interview Strategies ===


--- Data Lakes ---

1
So a lot of times when we're talking about big systems
2
and big data in particular,
3
you'll hear the term data lake thrown around
4
as opposed to a database.
5
What does that mean?
6
Well, one approach is instead of using a formal database,
7
just throw all of your raw data
8
into some big data repository somewhere,
9
some big distributed file storage system.
10
And maybe that data is just organized as log files
11
that are raw.
12
Maybe they're CSV files that has some structure to them.
13
Maybe they're JSON files
14
that have even more structure to them,
15
but it doesn't matter.
16
You just throw whatever data you have into a big old bucket.
17
And that's what we call a data lake.
18
Often, that's gonna be AWS S3 service
19
because that basically gives you infinite storage capacity,
20
where you just pay for the storage that you use.
21
And you can just keep on dumping raw data in there
22
to your heart's content.
23
So again, this is called the data lake
24
where you just have this huge repository of unstructured
25
data thrown into one big pile.
26
And that's a very common approach for big data applications
27
and where your data might be unstructured, for example,
28
a raw log file.
29
This is just being dumped in there somewhere.
30
Something however has to impart structure
31
upon that structureless data.
32
So in the case of the AWS Amazon web services,
33
they have a service called Amazon Glue,
34
whose job is to crawl that unstructured data
35
in your data lake and S3 in this case,
36
and sort of extract a schema around it
37
and allow you to define what that schema is.
38
So that allows you to query that data
39
that's just sitting in S3 is raw files
40
as if it were a database.
41
So this is a very different thing than like Mongo DB,
42
where you're actually distributing your data across this
43
fleet of highly redundant servers.
44
Instead, we're just actually hitting the data
45
in Amazon S3 in our distributed file system directly.
46
And we're just using this intermediate tool
47
to try to impart some structure onto that.
48
Now yes, the systems that you build on top of this
49
might do its own caching of that data and organize it
50
to be more performant, but fundamentally your data
51
is just being stored in some cloud storage service.
52
And it's redundant because it's storing multiple copies
53
of that across different regions
54
and different data centers automatically.
55
But really it's just a big data lake, a big file store
56
that you're querying as if it were a database.
57
So this is kind of a very common thing to do
58
in the world of big data or data analytics these days.
59
So once you have that Glue set up
60
that actually imparts that schema,
61
again going back to the example of Amazon Web Services,
62
Amazon Athena is an example of a serverless application
63
that allows you to query that data using SQL.
64
So that just is basically your SQL interface
65
to your data that's sitting in S3.
66
A step up would be Amazon Redshift.
67
And this is more of a distributed data warehouse.
68
In this case,
69
there's a thing called Redshift spectrum
70
that allows you to query S3 directly as opposed
71
to storing it in actual Redshift database servers.
72
And so Redshift is kind of this hybrid thing
73
between a more traditional data warehouse
74
and something sitting on top of a data lake
75
that's more akin to Amazon Athena.
76
So these are all just names I'm throwing out
77
as potential tools in your tool chest.
78
When you're being asked to design
79
some sort of a system around big data,
80
maybe these are things you can invoke in your solution.
81
Maybe if you just have a pile of logs
82
or a pile of CSV data coming in.
83
One approach would be to not use the database at all,
84
just throw it into S3,
85
use Glue to impart some structure on it,
86
and then query it using Amazon Athena,
87
using all these off the shelf tools
88
that don't even require you to design
89
how they work under the hood.
90
You just have to know that they are
91
in fact scalable and reliable.
92
Amazon worries about the details
93
so kind of cheating in that case.
94
You're not really doing the design,
95
but cheat to win sometimes.
96
If there's an off the shelf solution
97
or a technology that will do what you want,
98
you can at least mention it in your interview
99
and I think your interviewer will appreciate you
100
at least know that that's an option.
101
They might push back and ask you to design
102
something lower level or take a different route, so fine.
103
Do what they tell you to do,
104
but it never hurts to show that you know
105
that these things are out there, these technologies exist.
106
Now, even if you're gonna be using a data lake
107
or just throwing data into S3,
108
you still need to think about partitioning.
109
So for best performance,
110
you don't really want to throw everything
111
into one big bucket.
112
If you can organize it a little bit,
113
it will help a lot.
114
For example, if I'm storing a bunch of log data,
115
it would make sense
116
if I know I'm gonna be querying that data by date,
117
maybe a common use case
118
is to look at performance in the past day.

So if I know that's something I need to do
120
from my requirements that I'm gathering

for what users need to do with this data,
122
that might say that I want to partition this data by date.
123
So I can very quickly get at just the logs
124
for this given day and analyze those independently.
125
Or maybe I want to look at the logs for a given month.
126
So I could have this hierarchical partitioning scheme
127
where maybe I have a set of directories in my data store
128
for different years and each year's folder
129
has a bunch of month folders underneath it
130
and for each month there's a 30 or 31 or 28
131
or 29 day folders underneath that.
132
So if you can structure your data into a directory structure
133
that corresponds to partitions,
134
that can be a way to optimize performance
135
from the front end.
136
So we're gonna talk a lot about working backwards
137
from the customer and this is a good example of that.
138
You want to start by thinking about how are your end users
139
going to be using the data that you're storing?
140
How are they gonna be querying it?
141
What are the patterns of queries?
142
And that can lead you to design decisions
143
on how to best partition that data.
144
Again, common example, if I need to query
145
a given day's worth of data, I want to partition it by date.
146
If I wanna like query things by what store
147
they were bought in, maybe I want to partition by store.
148
I can do more than one too.
149
So here's what one architecture might look like,
150
getting back to a system design standpoint here.
151
Again, if I just wanted a big old pile of data sitting
152
in Amazon S3, maybe they're just log files.
153
Maybe I have those organized into sub-directories
154
for each year, month and day

so I can query them by day more efficiently.

AWS Glue would sit on top of that,

159
that can be read by Amazon Athena,
160
where I could just issue a SQL query

and treat it like a data warehouse.
162
And similarly with Amazon Redshift,

specifically Redshift Spectrum,

I could do the same thing using Redshift as well.


=== Mock Design Interviews ===


--- ACID compliance and the CAP theorem ---

1
We can't really talk about databases
2
without talking about ACID compliance.
3
You've probably heard of this before,
4
but I think it's worth a review.
5
There's a pretty good chance
6
that your assistant design interviewer
7
will at least want you to know what these concepts are
8
and they might ask about them.
9
So what does ACID compliance mean?
10
Well, the A stands for Atomicity.
11
That means that when you submit a query to your database,
12
the entire transaction succeeds,
13
or the entire thing fails.
14
In SQL, it's possible to have very complex statements.
15
You can include multiple transformations
16
or even apply complex functions to your data
17
as it's being submitted.
18
Atomicity means that everything that you want to do
19
has to be performed all at once.
20
It's an atomic operation.
21
So if any component of a write operation fails,
22
the entire thing fails and gets rolled back.
23
In an atomic situation,
24
we can't be in a situation where we only have partial data
25
written out or data that hasn't been fully transformed
26
the way that we wanted.
27
So when the database says that my command completed,
28
I know that it completed fully
29
and it wasn't left half finished due to some error
30
along the way.
31
There's no half-baked data getting written.
32
The C stands for Consistency.
33
In the context of ACID,
34
consistency means something totally different
35
than what we usually mean by the term.
36
It's not about immediately retrieving data
37
that was just written without any delays
38
for that data to be propagated through the system.
39
It's about consistently applying rules
40
to find within the database, totally different thing.
41
So if I have a rule that says this value
42
can never be negative,
43
and I try to write out a negative value somehow,
44
that transaction will be rolled back
45
to enforce consistency in applying that rule.
46
Now that sort of behavior is kind of a given these days.
47
So it's not something we think about too much.
48
It's very confusing because consistency,
49
in terms of the more widely used cap theorem,
50
which we'll get into next,
51
means something totally different.
52
So usually when we talk about consistency outside of ACID,
53
we're talking about how quickly can we get our data back
54
after we've written it.
55
And eventually consistent database
56
means that it takes time for changes to be written
57
throughout the system
58
and data that you write might not be read back immediately.
59
In big distributed databases,
60
where data needs to be replicated
61
across many different servers,
62
eventual consistency might be a trade-off
63
that you need to make.
64
So remember consistency can mean two different things,
65
in ACID it's about consistently applying the rules,
66
but everywhere else pretty much,
67
we're talking about consistently reading back
68
the data that we just wrote.
69
The I in ACID stands for Isolation.
70
And that means that no transaction is affected
71
by any other transaction that is still in progress.
72
So if you have two different commands running
73
that are fighting over the same data,
74
one of them's gonna win.
75
While one command is writing out some data,
76
another command cannot modify that data
77
while it's being written,
78
that could lead to some pretty weird states.
79
So you can see why isolation would be important
80
in most settings.
81
And finally, the D stands for Durability.
82
That just means that once the transaction is committed,
83
it's going to be there even if the system crashes.
84
It implies that you're writing this out
85
to some sort of durable storage, like a disc somewhere.
86
And if somebody pulls the plug on your server,
87
you won't lose your data.
88
Sometimes that's okay though.
89
And in memory, cache is not durable at all,
90
but it can still serve an important purpose
91
in accelerating applications while it's running.
92
But durability would be of utmost importance in a system
93
that's used for fulfilling orders
94
people paid good money for.
95
Big traditional relational database management systems
96
like Oracle, offer full ACID compliance.
97
But while these are all desirable traits in a database,
98
sometimes you have to give something up
99
in the name of scalability.
100
I'd like a pony and a unicorn too,
101
but I can't always have them.
102
And that's when we get into
103
what we call the CAP Theorem, okay.
104
So the CAP Theorem means
105
that there's three things that we really mostly care about
106
with databases: consistency, availability,
107
and what we're calling partition tolerance,
108
which is kind of like durability.
109
And the idea of the CAP Theorem
110
is that you can have any two of those, but not three.
111
So pick the two that you care about the most.
112
So let's look at some real examples here.
113
So for example,
114
a traditional database, like MySQL.
115
In that case,
116
it is highly available.
117
You know, we know there's like,
118
you know, there's not a bunch of servers that can go down.
119
We just have one host, as long as we keep that host happy,
120
our availability will be good.
121
Maybe there's a replicated host too for backup,
122
but you know,
123
compared to a system that has a single points of failure,
124
like a single host that routes commands
125
to a bunch of shards,
126
it's gonna be, generally speaking, more available.
127
It's also gonna be strongly consistent
128
because we don't have to replicate that data
129
all over the place in real time.
130
So, you know, I know that if I write some data,
131
I'm gonna get it back right away.
132
There's no question about that.
133
What I'm giving up is partition tolerance.
134
Very difficult to partition MySQL across
135
horizontally scaled fleets.
136
It actually is possible in modern MySQL,
137
the truth is the CAP Theorem is a lot weaker
138
than it used to be.
139
A lot of these modern databases actually do a good job
140
of giving you the best of all worlds.
141
Technically speaking,
142
they still have to give up on something,
143
but from a practical standpoint,
144
a lot of databases really give you all three
145
for all intents and purposes,
146
but this is what the CAP Theorem needs.
147
So if you do have to choose a specific database,
148
this is sort of a framework for which one
149
make might make sense for a given set of requirements.
150
So if availability and consistency are more important
151
than partition tolerance,
152
maybe I don't really care so much about scalability,
153
but I really, really, really need to have high availability
154
and consistent data.
155
An example might be, you know, gosh, I don't know,
156
maybe a little a small bank or something like that, right?
157
MySQL might be a good choice or Oracle.
158
On the other hand,
159
let's say that what I don't care about is consistency.
160
It's okay to me
161
if I write data and I don't get that data back immediately,
162
but I still want it to be very scalable.
163
I want to be partitioned tolerant,
164
and I want it to be highly available.
165
I don't want any single points of failure.
166
As we saw earlier, Cassandra fits that bill
167
because it has no single point of failure.
168
It has a sort of a ring structure
169
where any host can serve as the primary host.
170
So it's available.
171
It has high availability.
172
It's also partitioned tolerant
173
because I can either keep adding more hosts into that ring.
174
What I give up though is consistency
175
because as data's written,
176
that needs to be rewritten around that entire ring,
177
and that does not happen immediately.
178
So Cassandra is an example where I want availability
179
and partition tolerance, the A and the P,
180
and I'm giving up the C, the consistency.
181
However, most modern databases give up availability.
182
That might sound weird to you,
183
but really when we talk about availability
184
in the context of the CAP Theorem,
185
it's about avoiding single points of failure
186
that can go down.
187
And yes, most of these technologies
188
have a single point of failure that can go down.
189
So like in mongoDB,
190
we saw that there is a primary router host.
191
And if that goes down, there is gonna be some downtime
192
in the time that it takes for it to elect
193
a new replacement server to take its place.
194
Now in practical terms, that's gonna happen very quickly.
195
We do have a couple of a hot standbys
196
sitting there ready to go.
197
So as soon as the system detects that that primary routing
198
host went down,
199
it's gonna switch over to a new one
200
pretty much right away.
201
But technically speaking,
202
it's still a single point of failure.
203
There's still a chance of downtime when that happens,
204
even if it's for a very short period of time.
205
So this is usually the set of trade-offs that you make.
206
You're going to say, okay,
207
I'm willing to give up availability
208
if it means that, for a few seconds,
209
my system might go down.
210
I can live with that, a few seconds is okay.
211
But I really want consistency.
212
I really want partition tolerance.
213
And the thing that's generally non-negotiable
214
in these massive systems is partition tolerance.
215
We need to be able to scale these things out
216
as we throw more and more data
217
and more and more traffic at it.
218
So you see a whole bunch of stuff down
219
on that side of the triangle.
220
HBase, mongoDB,
221
Amazon DynamoDB is an example of that as well.
222
Amazon DynamoDB actually has different modes
223
that you can put it in. So I'm saying here,
224
if I have strongly consistent mode,
225
it's gonna be down in this part of the triangle,
226
but actually there's a switch there
227
where I can decide,
228
do I care about consistency or not?
229
So maybe I could put that on a different side
230
of the triangle instead.
231
So again, a lot of these modern database systems
232
allow you to make your own trade-offs
233
and choose which of which side of the CAP triangle
234
you want to be on, okay?
235
So that's the CAP Theorem in a nutshell.
236
Consistency, do I get back what I just wrote right away?
237
Availability?
238
Do I have any single points of failure that can go down?
239
And partition tolerance.
240
Can I horizontally scale the system easily.
241
Again, in practical terms,
242
the trade-offs aren't what they used to be,
243
but you gotta pick one.


=== General Tech Interview Tips ===


--- Using CAP to Choose a Database ---

1
All right, so just to reinforce the CAP theorem
2
let's dive a little bit more deeply again
3
into some of those examples that we talked about.
4
So again, from MongoDB what we're trading off
5
is the A part of CAP, the availability.
6
And the reason being
7
that we have a single point of failure here.
8
Actually, we have a couple of them, right?
9
So we have a config server, that's the primary,
10
and we have an app server,
11
that is the primary at any given time.
12
Now even though we have multiple backups
13
sitting there waiting to go,
14
there is gonna be a brief period of downtime
15
should any one of those servers go down.
16
You know, what happens is the system has to first detect
17
that there was an outage,
18
and then the remaining servers need
19
to elect a new primary host to take its place.
20
And that can take a few seconds.
21
Those few seconds are enough to say
22
that what we're trading off here is availability
23
because we still have consistency,
24
because all of our data is being written consistently
25
to a given replica set.
26
And we still have partition tolerance
27
because I can add as many replica sets as I need
28
and partition this data to my heart's content
29
to make it scalable.
30
Again for Cassandra,
31
what we're giving up here is the C in the CAP theorem,
32
the consistency.
33
There is no single master in Cassandra.
34
So there is no way that,
35
you know unless every node went down at once,
36
that you're gonna have an availability issue here.
37
But that means that we need to replicate this data
38
across all the nodes as we go.
39
And because of that it's eventually consistent.
40
Now technically speaking it's not all the nodes,
41
you know, it can still distribute the data in ways
42
that make sense that let it scale up better.
43
But, you know fundamentally the data for a given shard
44
needs to be replicated to a node somewhere else.
45
And there's gonna be some time in which that happens.
46
So because there's no single master,
47
we have to trade off consistency because it might take time
48
for that data to be replicated to whatever node
49
that data's being backed up on.
50
So the moral of the story here is if you're being asked
51
to choose a database technology
52
as part of your system design,
53
make sure you understand the requirements about scaling,
54
the partition tolerance,
55
the consistency and the availability
56
before you propose a specific database solution.
57
Make sure you ask these questions.
58
You know, what do I care about
59
in the requirements for the system?
60
Is it consistency, is it availability?
61
Is it partition tolerance?
62
If you say availability is important, how important?
63
You know, is a few seconds of downtime okay?
64
If so, then I can use something
65
that actually trades off availability,
66
if it's only a few seconds of downtime
67
that we're talking about.
68
Generally speaking,
69
something like DynamoDB will fit the bill.
70
Something like MongoDB will fit the bill.
71
You know, those are pretty general purpose solutions,
72
but it's always gonna score some points
73
with your interviewer if you can talk intelligently
74
about the trade-offs you're making
75
by choosing a specific database technology.


=== Good Luck on your Interview! ===


--- Caching: Introduction ---

1
(jolly synth music)
2
Not only do your systems need to be scalable,
3
they also need to be fast.
4
You'll be held to SLA's, service level agreements,
5
that specify that requests to your underlying services
6
respond in a matter of milliseconds almost all of the time.
7
To do that, we need to employ caching technologies
8
at just the right places.
9
Avoiding disk seeks and long trips across the Internet
10
are key to fast performance.
11
In this section,
12
we'll discuss several caching strategies and technologies
13
you can invoke in your system design
14
to show that you know how to make systems
15
that scale and perform well.

--- Caching Technologies ---

1
So any time we're talking about making
2
a highly performance and highly scalable system,
3
caching is probably gonna be
4
a part of the solution, right?
5
So hitting disk is really expensive.
6
You want to avoid hitting discs in your database
7
or whatever data store you have as much as possible.
8
And one way to do that is caching
9
that database information in memory,
10
inside a cache of some sort.
11
So let's talk about some caching strategies
12
and how that works.
13
We'll start with sort of an uncached architecture here.
14
One that just hits the database directly.
15
So to review here, we have our clients
16
at the top, communicating through the internet,
17
talks to a load balancer.
18
That might be a hardware device,
19
might be software running on a service fleet of its own.
20
But basically that load balancer
21
is distributing that traffic
22
to a horizontally distributed fleet
23
of application servers or web servers,
24
depending on what you're doing.
25
Now in this diagram,
26
they're all talking to one big fat database.
27
So there is a big old bottleneck, right?
28
If I'm gonna be hitting the disc
29
from all of those distributed app servers,
30
that could bring the database to its knees.
31
I've only got so many hard drives, right?
32
And so many disc heads, they can only do so much at once.
33
So those disc kits are gonna be expensive.
34
And usually the database will have
35
its own caching internally inside the database itself.
36
But that might not be enough, right?
37
There's only so much memory you can put on one host.
38
And even if that's the distributed database system,
39
which we'll talk about shortly,
40
it would be beneficial to have a cache closer
41
to the application hosts themselves.
42
Remember these all might be living
43
in different data centers or whatnot.
44
So it can be beneficial to avoid a hop to that data center,
45
even to hit that database, if you can help it.
46
So the way, or one way to deal with that
47
is by introducing a caching layer.
48
And what is that thing?
49
Well, that is probably a fleet of other servers
50
that are sitting alongside your application servers,
51
whose only job is to keep in-memory copies
52
of data that's been going to that database.
53
So it can keep track of the most recent hits
54
to the database or the most popular ones,
55
and keep that cached in memory,
56
which is gonna be a lot faster to access
57
than hitting the disc
58
in your big old bottleneck database there.
59
And another advantage of that is that

of our application fleet, if we want to.

69
of the application hosts.

So that is one way to severely improve your performance
74
when hitting the database,

just keep it in-memory.

How do they work?

Like I said, it's usually

a horizontally scaled fleet of some sort,

Another problem is hotspots.

--- Eviction Strategies for Caching ---

1
So let's talk a little bit more about eviction policies.
2
How a cache actually decides
3
what stays in and what gets kicked out.
4
You only have a finite amount of memory
5
in your caching layer.
6
So if you can't fit everything into your cache,
7
you're gonna have to make some decisions
8
about what stays in the cache
9
and what actually results in a database hit.
10
That's what we call a cache miss, right?
11
So if it's not actually being stored
12
in your cache currently,
13
that's gonna flow right through to the database
14
and repopulate the cache,
15
maybe with that information on a cache miss.
16
So there's at least three ways of handling this.
17
Probably the most common is called an LRU cache,
18
or a least recently used algorithm for the eviction policy.
19
The idea there is that we evict
20
the least recently used item from the cache.
21
So we keep track of which most recently used items
22
are in the cache.
23
So as we request something from the cache,
24
that basically goes to the front of the list,
25
and as we request something else from the cache,
26
that goes to the front end list in front of the other thing.
27
And all the time, the least frequently used,
28
the thing that we haven't looked at
29
in the most amount of time
30
is gonna be at the end of our list.
31
So if we run out of space,
32
that's where we're gonna start kicking stuff
33
out of the cache to make room for other things.
34
So pretty simple concept,
35
least recently used eviction policy means the oldest thing
36
that I've looked at is gonna be kicked out
37
if I need space in my cache or LRU for short.
38
And over on the right here,
39
we have sort of a sample data architecture
40
for how that might work.
41
So one way of doing that is by maintaining a HashMap
42
for all of our keys and all of those keys
43
point to where that value is stored within the cache itself.
44
So maybe key one points to item slot number two,
45
and key two points to item slot number one,
46
and so on and so forth.
47
But the key is that as I access these things,
48
I'm gonna be moving those items around
49
in a doubly linked list.
50
So as I access something, I'm gonna move it to the head.
51
So let's say that I just access key number two
52
in this example, that got rearranged
53
in my doubly linked list to the front of my list.
54
That means that I know that
55
that is the most recently used item.
56
And by doing that over time,
57
the least recently used item would just bubbled down
58
to the end automatically, right?
59
And I might actually keep a separate pointer to that
60
as part of this process where I can quickly point
61
to where the tail is.
62
So as a new item gets pushed to the end,
63
I'm gonna update the tail pointer to that.
64
And if that thing does get access to move to the front,
65
I'll update the tail to point to the new tail, right?
66
So, I'm not gonna get into the code
67
of how you might do that,
68
but that's algorithmically the idea there.
69
I've just had this linked list where I'm always moving
70
the most recently accessed thing to the front
71
and the least recently accessed thing to the end,
72
and I have a head pointer and a tail pointer that allows me
73
to quickly reference either the head
74
or the tail of that list.
75
So if I need to evict something,
76
I just look at where the tail pointer is,
77
dispose off that thing, get rid of its memory
78
and move the tail thing to whatever it was before it.
79
If I access something, I'm gonna move it to the front.
80
And that's where the head pointer will come in handy
81
for quickly adding things to the front of the list.
82
So we'll talk more about data structures,
83
but hopefully you have enough background in computer science
84
to know what a HashMap and a linked list are.
85
Basically that's how an LRU cache works.
86
Very simple thing.
87
And actually this can be a design problem on its own.
88
Design in LRU hash is kind of a common interview question
89
in its own, right?
90
So, hey, there's your extra bonus
91
interview question answer right there.
92
A couple of other ways to do it.
93
And one is least frequently use.
94
So this is where we actually keep track
95
of how often a given key is hit over some period of time.
96
Little bit more complicated, right?
97
There you can see how that might be a little bit
98
more predictive of what might make sense
99
to actually keep in the cache or not.
100
From a practical standpoint,
101
LRU usually works well enough at scale
102
assuming you have a large enough cache,
103
but if you have a small one,
104
maybe LFU would be the way to go
105
if you need to be a little bit more picky
106
about what stays and what goes.
107
And finally, there's FIFO, first in first out.
108
Again, just sounds like a what it is.
109
So the first thing that I put into my cache
110
is gonna be the first thing that gets evicted later on.
111
All right, let's talk about a few

--- Content Distribution Networks (CDN's) ---

1

2

Definitely a piece of your application
5

6
So again, let's come back to our diagram here
7

So let's say we have our clients

23

24

25

26

27
If I'm trying to hit a server in Virginia from Japan,
28

30

31

46

48

69

70

82
They call these availability zones
83
where they guarantee that, you know,
84
at least you can think of this as one entity.

And finally an entire region could potentially go out.

So what happens if somebody cuts
87
the underground underwater cable
88
connecting America to Europe?

You know, of course there's more than one in practice,
90
but you need to plan for the possibility of an entire region
91
going down as well.
92
You never know.
93
And you know, if there's anything more
94
than a whole region going down,

well, you know, you've got bigger problems.

It's time to start thinking about survival.

So we're not going to think anything beyond that,
98
you know, meteor strikes the Earth.
99
You're going to be worried about more than your job
100
at that point,

but these are the things you need to plan for.
102
So how do you do that?
103
So let's break up our sort of traditional diagram here,
104
where we have a load balancer.
105
And instead of just having one load balancer
106
for our entire application,
107
we're actually doing some sort of geo routing.
108
So one way of handling this is that,

to actually figure out where do I send a request to locally.
111
So that way, traffic from North America
112

114

117
And all of my India traffic is getting routed

119
So this gives me some more resiliency, right?
120
Because if all of Europe went down,
121

If I had to.

So if that geo routing system there could detect

that a region went down, it could automatically say,

because Europe is not responding for whatever reason,

hope everyone's okay, but it's all right.

We'll just reroute data to North America or India,
129
whatever the closest region might be.
130
And that will be a little bit slower,
131
but it's better than going down, right?
132
This does mean that you need to have enough excess capacity
133
in your system to handle that happening.
134
So, you know, it might be reasonable
135
to assume that no more than one
136
region will go down at a time.
137
So that means that you need to take the entire capacity
138
of that region and make sure the remaining regions
139
have enough excess capacity to handle that traffic,
140
if the need arises.
141
So we need to like overscale things in order
142
to make sure that we can be resilient
143
and handle the entire failure of an entire region.
144
So we need to be smart about how we distribute our servers
145
is what I'm getting at here.
146
Your secondary backup servers need to be spread
147
across multiple racks, right?
148
Because if you have all of your backup data in one rack
149
and that rack goes down, you're out of luck.
150
So you want to be spread out across multiple racks
151
for your backup servers.
152
You want to be spread out across multiple availability zones
153
in case an entire data center goes out,
154
and you want to be spread out across multiple regions
155
in case an entire region goes out.
156
And like I said,
157
you've got to make sure your system
158
has enough excess capacity
159
to handle any one of those failures at any reasonable scale.
160
That means that you need to have more servers on hand
161
than you technically need,
162
because you need to handle the possibility
163
of a bunch of them failing all at once.
164
Now you know, this isn't carved in stone necessarily.
165
It does come down to your requirements.

Again, you want to ask questions
167

173
So not every system warrants
174

175
You need to think about what is the worst case scenario
176
that you want to be able to handle,
177
and what are you willing to pay for it
178
at the end of the day.
179
Now in a place like Amazon or Google or Facebook,
180
they're going to be willing to pay any price to do that
181
because they basically have infinite money.
182
They cannot tolerate any downtime.
183
So they're going to want to pay the extra money
184
to have that excess capacity in place.
185
So when you're asked to develop a really massive system,
186
usually the answer is, you just over-provision
187
and not really think about it too hard.
188
But it's worth asking.
189
You know, it's always good to demonstrate
190

191
the trade-offs there.
192
And you know, this is going to cost money
193
and cost more servers to maintain and operate
194
by being able to have enough excess capacity
195
to handle a region failure.
196
So yeh, when you're thinking about resiliency,
197
it's all about distributing your servers
198
across different racks, data centers, regions,
199
so that you have backups of all of your data
200
and all of your systems across those different zones.
201
And making sure you understand that you're over-provisioning
202
to handle the failure of any one of those entities.

--- HDFS Architecture ---

1
So designing a distributed storage solution
2
might be fair game for system design interview question
3
of its own.
4
So why don't we dive into a little bit more depth
5
as to how one actually works?
6
So let's talk about how HDFS works in particular
7
as an example of a distributed storage system
8
under the hood.
9
HDFS, again, is the Hadoop distributed file system.
10
It's part of the Hadoop,
11
which you might deploy in your own server fleet
12
within your own data center.
13
So it's an open source system.
14
So the way that HDFS works is that files are broken up
15
into blocks of some size.
16
I forget the size,
17
it's like 128 megabytes or something like that.
18
And these blocks of data get replicated
19
across your entire cluster.
20
So for any given block,
21
we're going to have a copy of it somewhere else,
22
and another copy somewhere else.
23
Right?
24
And if we're smart about it,
25
we'll be rack aware so that we're not going to be storing
26
the backups of your blocks in the same rack.
27
That would be bad.
28
It's like we talked about,
29
we want to be smart about how we distribute the backups
30
of your data,
31
such that we can be the most fault tolerant
32
and the most resilient.
33
So how do we know where to go to get that data?
34
So let's say, you know, we're hitting HDFS and say,
35
hey, I want to retrieve the contents of this file.
36
There has to be some master node that knows where the blocks
37
of that file are stored and where to get them from.
38
And that's what this master name node is.
39
So this name node in HDFS,
40
you can just call it a master node if you want,
41
coordinates all those operations.
42
So a client will hit the name node and say,
43
hey, I want to get this data.
44
The name node will come back and say, okay,
45
here's the nearest replica to your client server.
46
This is going to give you the best performance
47
for getting that data back.
48
So it's going to direct it to the block that should be able
49
to access most quickly.
50
And ideally it might even be hosted on the same server.
51
So a nice thing about Hadoop is that if you're actually
52
running your application on the same host as your data,
53
as your HDFS system,
54
it will try to store the data in the actual same partition
55
that a given server is actually deployed to.
56
Let me talk about that in a little bit more detail.
57
So let's imagine that you have some distributed system
58
that's analyzing a lot of data,
59
so likely partitioning the processing of a given set
60
of that data to one server host and HDFS would actually
61
try to make sure that the data for that partition
62
is hosted locally on that same host
63
when it's distributing up what blocks get written where
64
for the best performance.
65
So like I said,
66
those rights will get replicated across different racks.
67
And for high availability situations,
68
there might be three or more name nodes to fall back on.
69
And the name nodes also have to have some sort of a data
70
store that keeps track of where the replicas are,
71
where all the block locations are and whatnot.
72
We call that our metadata store
73
and that too might have a backup host as well.
74
So the problem with an architecture like this is that it has
75
a single master node, right?
76
That name node.
77
You have to be able to go somewhere to find out where
78
the data is stored for a given file,
79
where those blocks are distributed from, right?
80
That can be a single point of failure.
81
So we need to have some sort of a smarts in and HDFS to say,
82
okay, if my name node goes down,
83
I've got at least two other backups sitting there
84
waiting to go,
85
and they can elect a new primary node that our clients
86
will start to talk to.
87
So we need to make sure that the other two surviving
88
name nodes can still talk to our clients and tell them,
89
hey, don't use that dead name mode anymore, use me instead.
90
And that makes it a little bit more resilient.
91
So even though technically that's a single point of failure,
92
we have a mechanism in place to make sure that our system
93
will only be down for a few seconds in the event
94
of a primary node failure and the same thing
95
for the metadata storage as well.
96
We need to make sure that that's backed up
97
and highly available as well.
98
So that's how HDFS works in a nutshell.
99
Basically our clients communicate
100
with a central name node server.
101
That's backed up by a metadata data store
102
that keeps track of what files are stored in what blocks
103
and where those blocks are stored.
104
And it's also smart about when we write data,
105
distributing that data across multiple racks,
106
multiple regions, what have you,
107
to make sure that we have backups
108
of all the data somewhere else.
109
So if any given server goes down,
110
the name node will know where to go to get the backup copy
111
of that data instead.
112
So this way we have a system that's very resilient
113
because we have backups of everything everywhere.
114
And it's also very efficient,
115
it's very fast because we're trying to localize
116
where that data is stored to where that data
117
is being processed.
118
And we've distributed that data across different racks.
119
So even if it's not the same host,
120
odds are good that I can access that data from within
121
the same rack as well.
122
So that's also going to be super fast.
123
So an example of a distributed storage system,
124
where we're breaking up files into blocks,
125
we're being smart about where we store those blocks
126
to make sure that they're distributed in such a way
127
that we can get at them quickly from any server,
128
from any client.
129
And we have backups in place for that central name node
130
and for the metadata,
131
just in case something happens to them.

--- Linked Lists ---

1
All right,
2
let's go into a review of algorithms and data structures,
3
kind of the computer science fundamental stuff here.
4
And even though sometimes you'll get a separate interview
5
questions specifically aimed at algorithms and data
6
structures.
7
Oftentimes you'll need to invoke them in the context of a
8

9
So I think it's worth reviewing them here.
10
The intent of this section is not to teach you all of
11
algorithms and data structures from scratch.
12
I'm assuming you've encountered this stuff before,
13
and you just need to review before going into your
14
interview.
15
Obviously this entire books and courses around computer
16
science,
17
but if you're like me and you originally learn computer
18

maybe this is a gap in your knowledge that you really need
20

Even if in practical terms,

from scratch in the real world these days. Right?
26
But still good to know about
27
Let's dive in and we'll start
28
with a link lists.
29
The most basic flavor of a link list is a singly linked
30
list.
31
And the idea of a link list is that it can grow dynamically.
32
So as opposed to an array where you're statically allocating
33
a block of memory and dividing that up amongst a bunch of
34
objects that you want to store a link list can grow
35
dynamically.
36
So you're never using more memory than you need to,
37
although you have to maintain these pointers and those
38
pointers take memory too something to think about.
39
But generally the idea is that you start with some sort of a
40
head, you have a pointer to the head of your list.
41
And as you add things to your list,
42
you just have a pointer on each node
43
that's pointing to the next thing in the list.
44
And at the end of the list, you're just pointing to null.
45
And that's how, you know, you're at the end.
46
The access in this case is what we call O of N
47
so big O notation. What we call that.
48
We're basically saying,
49
what is the order of actually doing something,
50
accessing something,
51
looking something up to leading something,
52
inserting something,
53
whatever it is.
54
In this case access is what we say O of N.
55
That means that if we N items in our linked list,
56
it could take up to N iterations to find that thing.
57
So if we start at the head,
58
we just need to March through this list,
59
one element at a time going through all those next pointers
60
until we find the thing we're looking for.
61
Not the most efficient algorithm for a search, right?
62
So that's why we call it O of N.
63
Now, when I was first learning about big O notation,
64
I found it really confusing because I think in practical
65
terms, right?
66
So practically, N is the worst case scenario here,
67
Um you're pretty unlikely to actually hit that. You know,
68
on the average, it's probably more like N over two,
69
but you can't overthink these things. We're just thinking,
70
you know, what is the order of operation here?
71
Big O order, it's hard to explain,
72
but basically if we have a linear list of things that we
73
need to go through to find something, we would say that as,
74
O of N
75
Now an insert at the head in contrast would just be,
76
O of one, because we have this handy-dandy head pointer.
77
It's only a single operation to tack something new onto the
78
head. We just need to create that object,
79
put the next pointer to the current head and change our head
80
pointer to that new object, done one operation.
81
And again, in practical terms,
82
there's actually three things you had to do there,
83
but that's not how we think about it.
84
We think about it in terms of how many elements did I need
85
to visit potentially to do what I want to do
86
Inserting at the end, however, is definitely
87
O of N right.
88
Cause I'm going to have to iterate through every single
89
thing in this list,
90
following those next pointers until I reached the end by
91
finding a null at the next pointer.
92
And now I know I'm at the end.
93
So inserting at the end is unambiguously O of N.
94
I really do need to go through N items and a list of
95
N things to do an insert at the end.
96
So what do you use these things for obviously use cases that
97
involve sequential access as sort of the primary thing you
98
want to do would be a good case of using a linked list,
99
a singly linked list.
100
If I just need a March through things in some given order,
101
like the order in which they were received,
102
a linked list might do the job there.
103
So this is useful for things like stacks and queues,
104
but it's especially useful if you're also keeping track of
105
the tail. You know,
106
if you need to keep track of both least recently,
107
and most recently used things, you kind of want both.
108
And, you know,
109
you could maintain a tail pointer as part of a singly linked
110
list,
111
but it's gotta be a little bit more of a natural fit for a
112
doubly linked place, which we'll talk about next.
113
Another good advantage though,
114
of a singly link list is that it does use less memory than a
115

116

117
but there is no previous pointer.
118
So if memory is at a premium,
119
which these days is usually not the case,
120

121
The doubly linked list in contrast
122
has both a next and a previous
123
pointer for every node.
124
So I can go in either direction very easily.
125
So an insert at the front or the back in this case is going
126
to be O of one,
127
because I do have a tail pointer in addition to a head
128

129

130

131
And going ahead and inserting something right there.
132
Access however is still considered O of N because I would
133

134

135
If I do have some way of making an educated guess as to
136
whether I'll read something more quickly from the head or
137
the tail, I will,
138
I can make a better choice of where to start searching from
139

140
This is useful for something called a Deques
141
or a double-ended
142
queue because that's exactly what it is. Double-ended.
143
We have a head and a tail and we can traverse through things
144
in either direction.
145
This is also commonly used for MRU,
146
most recently used caches.
147
It just makes it easy to move things around. So if I have a,
148
a given item that I've accessed,
149
because I have that next in previous pointer,
150
it's very easy for me to connect the things around it back
151

152
So that's a very quick thing to do with a doubly linked
153
list,
154
because if I need to pluck something out of it and move it
155
to the head, having that next and previous pointer,
156
