--- Binary Trees and Hash Tables ---

1
All right, next let's talk about binary trees,
2
and binary search trees in particular.
3
This is a sample of what one might look like.
4
So instead of having a next and a previous pointer
5
pointing to the next and previous things in the list,
6
a tree is well, a tree structure.
7
So, the idea here is that every element
8
instead has a left and a right child pointer.
9
So we have the ability
10
to work our way down through this tree,
11
looking at the left and right children of each note
12
as we work our way down.
13
Now, if left and right are ordered,
14
meaning that things that are less than the thing
15
that I'm looking at go to the left,
16
and things that are greater than,
17
go to the right.
18
Then we call this a binary search tree
19
because it is an ordered binary tree.
20
And this is very useful for search. Right?
21
Now less than and greater than
22
doesn't mean that it has to be a number necessarily.
23
It just means that we have to have some consistent way
24
of saying, well, this value is greater than,
25
or less than this value. Maybe that's based on, you know,
26
alphabetical sort of strings or something like that.
27
It doesn't have to be a number necessarily.
28
So access in this case, is log(n). Why is that?
29
Well, if we have a perfectly balanced binary tree,
30
you can see that as we work our way down,
31
we're basically dividing our search space in half
32
every time.
33
So we start off with a half of the stuff on the left,
34
and half of the stuff on the right.
35
As soon as we pick a direction there,
36
because we know the thing we're looking for
37
is either less than, or greater than
38
the thing at the top of the tree.
39
We've instantly cut our search space in half.
40
And as we work our way down to the next level,
41
we do it again. Right?
42
So, here we would say, okay, well,
43
the thing I'm looking for is either less than,
44
or greater than this thing that I'm at,
45
I'm going to pick one direction and now I've again,
46
cut my search space in half yet again.
47
So we've gone from the entire search space,
48
to half of the search space,
49
to half of half of the search space.
50
And I could keep on going.
51
That is a logarithmic relationship,
52
which is why we say that access
53
is a O{log(n)} on average.
54
However,
55
there was a worst case scenario here
56
that you need to know about as well.
57
So, if I were to insert things into a binary search tree
58
in sorted order,
59
this would degenerate to a link list.
60
And that's going to be O(n),
61
as you might recall. Think about it, right?
62
If I like start with the number one and I answer it,
63
the number two, that's going to go to the right.
64
And if I then insert the number three,
65
that will go to the right again,
66
number four would go to the right again.
67
Five would go to the right again.
68
So, if I'm inserting things in sorted order,
69
I'm just going to end up with a singly linked list.
70
And that's not a particularly efficient structure
71
for searching for things.
72
So, it's good to know, that log(n)
73
represents the best case scenario, or the average scenarios,
74
how we say it in computer science world.
75
But O(n) is really the worst case scenario.
76
So in practical terms, it's good to know that, right?
77
Especially if you're dealing with sorta data
78
from the get-go.
79
So, sometimes theory and reality are a little bit different,
80
and it's good to speak to that.
81
Insertions and deletions are also log(n) again,
82
in the best case, and O(n) in the worst case.
83
Because you have to do a search to find out
84
how you're going to rearrange things. All right.
85
So, really no different there.
86
So, sometimes you'll see implementations
87
of binary search trees, where they are self-balancing.
88
So there are algorithms out there
89
that will rearrange the tree.
90
As you insert things into it automatically,
91
to try to keep it balanced.
92
This is useful in situations
93
where you're not doing a lot of inserts,
94
or maybe you just do the inserts upfront,
95
but what you're really doing quickly and at massive scale
96
are those lookups later on.
97
So if you really want to optimize for fast look-ups,
98
a self-balancing binary tree might be the way to go.
99
So, mostly this is used in cases
100
where you need to do an in-order traversal
101
of the stuff that you're putting in.
102
So it is possible to write a pretty simple
103
recursive algorithm that goes through everything
104
in a binary search tree and prints them out in sorted order.
105
You basically just work your way down all the way
106
to the left and go up a step, go to the right, you know,
107
and just recursively go back up, and just go through,
108
and search through as you get things in sorted order.
109
So, this is not a coding interview class.
110
We're talking about design interviews,
111
but a very common coding question
112
is implement a binary search tree in order traversal.
113
And that's something that's easily done using recursion.
114
So, if you want a little practice for your coding skills,
115
that's a good one to try.
116
And an incredibly useful,
117
and incredibly common data structure
118
in the world of system design, is the hash table.
119
Why is that?
120
It allows us to take a key that we're looking for
121
that has some value, some data associated with it,
122
and very quickly figure out where that data is stored.
123
So in the case of a hash table,
124
that's just a data structure sitting on one machine.
125
We might have some sort of a hash function
126
applied to our keys,
127
that maps those hash functions to a bucket of some sort.
128
So we have some fixed number of buckets
129
that we're storing stuff within.
130
And our hash function allows you to very quickly
131
through a single mathematical operation,
132
map that key to which bucket
133
that keys data is stored within.
134
So, in this particular example here,
135
the key A is being mapped to bucket number one,
136
through that hash function. So is key C somehow.
137
So that also maps to bucket one.
138
You can have less buckets than keys.
139
In fact, usually you will.
140
B might be mapped to the second one,
141
and D might be mapped to the third one.
142
So, once we actually know what bucket we're in,
143
we will search that bucket for the specific key
144
that we're looking for and its value.
145
So if it's more than one thing,
146
we might have to do a little bit of a traversal
147
to find it within that bucket.
148
But the idea is that we split this up
149
in such a way that these individual buckets
150
don't have a whole lot of data to go through,
151
to look, to look at.
152
So they can be very, very efficient.
153
Now, when you do have this case where a more than one key
154
points to the same bucket, we call that a hash collision.
155
And in that case, you know,
156
we have to have some sort of a strategy
157
for how do I find multiple things within the same bucket.
158
Often it will just be a link list like you see here,
159
but if we know that there's gonna be a lot
160
of hash collisions, you might want something more efficient.
161
For example, a binary search tree,
162
like we just talked about, is another alternative.
163
So big O notation, in this case, technically, inserts,
164
lookups, and deletions, or order of one,
165
because in the common average case,
166
you apply your hash function to your key,
167
and you go directly to the bucket where that data is stored.
168
So if you don't have any hash collisions,
169
that truly is an order one operation. However, again,
170
there's the worst case scenario to think about.
171
So if I have everything mapping to the same bucket,
172
and if my,
173
my hash function is absolutely terrible and inefficient,
174
and I've mapped things to a link list.
175
Well, in the worst case,
176
I might have O(n) for a lookup instead,
177
or an insert or deletion. Okay.
178
So again,
179
it's all in the implementation, tuning those hash functions,
180
and how big,
181
how many buckets you have is pretty crucial
182
to good performance.
183
But why is this particularly important for system design?
184
Well,
185
it's also a useful tool for distributing lookups
186
across a whole fleet of servers.
187
So if I have a horizontally partitioned database,
188
or data store, like a no sequel data store,
189
I could just have a hash function that says, okay,
190
you want this key?
191
I'm going to use that hash function to figure out
192
which machine in my whole cluster, that data is stored on.
193
So the whole idea of a hash function
194
can be used it's at two levels, right?
195
So, I might have one function
196
that maps to which machine my data is stored on.
197
And within that machine,
198
there might be another hash table,
199
that points to where that data
200

201
So, extremely useful tool in the context of system design.
202
So anytime you need fast look-ups,
203
which is usually the case,
204

--- Graphs and Graph Traversal ---

1
Let's also touch on graphs.
2
If you're being asked to design a social network,
3
or something, this might come up as well.
4
So the idea of a graph is that we have a bunch of nodes
5
or vertices, technically they're usually called vertices,
6
that represent some sort of entity,
7
maybe it's a person in a social network,
8
to go with that example,
9
and they are connected together
10
in arbitrary ways through edges.
11
So, in the case of a social network,
12
the vertices might represent people
13
and the edges might represent friendships
14
or, you know, connections between those people.
15
So other examples, besides the social network,
16
could be paths in a city.
17
Maybe these are, you know, actual physical connections
18
or networks in general, and even computer networks,
19
any sort of network, in the computer science sense,
20
can be represented by a graph data structure.
21
So the main thing to know about graphs
22
are the two different traversal strategies.
23
One is called Breadth-First-Search, or BFS,
24
and one is called Depth-First-Search, or DFS.
25
So BFS tends to be the more common one.
26
That's where you actually just, kinda like,
27
start at one end and work your way down recursively.
28
So in a Breadth-First-Search I might start here,
29
at the beginning, and then I'd explore
30
both the left and the right nodes from there.
31
Basically anything connected to that node directly,
32
there might be more than two,
33
because this isn't a binary tree it's a graph,
34
it could have anything, right.
35
Maybe I have more than two friends.
36
And, you know, after I explore that and I explore that,
37
I start to explore all the connections of those connections.
38
So, from here, I might then go to this connection here,
39
and, from there, I would then explore this node
40
and this node, and, from there, I'd say, okay, well,
41
that's a dead end, but I need to explore this one further,
42
let's go check that out,
43
and, so, that's why we call it Breadth-First.
44
You know, we're kinda like working our way down
45
one level at a time through each connection
46
until we get to the end, that kind of last row,
47
or, in this case, column of our graph.
48
Depth-First-Search, in contrast,
49
works its way all the way down one path,
50
all the way to the end, and then works its way back up
51
and tries a different path, right.
52
So I might go do-do-do-do all the way to the end,
53
work its way back up, go do-do-do-do down to that path,
54
work its way back up, and then explore that path,
55
so that would be Depth-First-Search.
56
Which one you choose kinda depends on the nature
57
of your data, but in the terms of massive datasets,
58
like a social network or crawling the web,
59
these things tend to be way too deep
60
for Depth-First-Search to be practical, you know,
61
the whole benefit of Breadth-First-Search
62
is that it's a little bit more easy
63
to parallelize that and distribute it.
64
So, you know, if you're given the question
65
should you do BFS or DFS,
66
and you have to guess, it's probably BFS,
67
but the reason being if the structure you're given
68
is too deep, that's gonna be hard to traverse
69
in a Depth-First manner.
70
Big O notation access is technically V + E.
71
That means the sum of the number of vertices
72
and the number of edges that you would need to access
73
to actually find something
74
'cause, you know, this isn't ordered or anything.
75
There's no way to cheat and find a specific thing
76
within this graph, you actually do have to traverse
77
the whole thing to find a given item if you need to,
78
and to find a given thing, to traverse it,
79
you need to visit every vertex,
80
and you need to visit every edge in the process.

--- Search Algorithms ---

1
Let's switch gears from data structures
2
to algorithms here,
3
and talk about a couple of common search algorithms
4
for finding something in a data structure.
5
Linear search, kind of the most basic one.
6
We talked about this already
7
in the context of a linked list.
8
Idea here is that you start with an array or a list.
9
It doesn't have to be sorted.
10
In this example, it is, but it doesn't have to be.
11
Cause to find something in a linked list,
12
we need to do a linear search,
13
where we just start at the beginning
14
and work our way through it,
15
until we find what we're looking for.
16
So obviously that is O(n),
17
you know, for n items
18
we could potentially have to go through all n of them,
19
looking for the thing that we want,
20
because we're just visiting them one at a time,
21
kind of hoping for the best.
22
So probably not the best choice
23
if search is important to you.
24
A little bit of a better approach is binary search.
25
So in this case, we start off by sorting our data.
26
So, sometimes it's pretty quick to sort things.
27
It could be an array, it could be a list.
28
I don't really care.
29
But the idea is that because it's sorted,
30
I can start at the middle,
31
in kind of the same way we did a binary search tree.
32
I know that if I start at the middle,
33
everything to the left of that will be less than that value
34
and everything to the right will be greater than that value.
35
Right? So immediately I've cut my search space in half.
36
Let's say, in this example, I'm looking for the value 123.
37
If I sort everything in my array or my list ahead of time,
38
I can pick the middle.
39
You know, kind of arbitrary
40
in the case of a even number of items,
41
what do you call the middle?
42
But have some convention for that.
43
Let's say 23 is the middle.
44
The thing I'm looking for is to the right of that,
45
because I know 123 is greater than 23.
46
So let's take the remainder of that list
47
from the right of 23.
48
That's just these three items here.
49
And I'll just start
50
in the middle of that remaining list there: 67.
51
I say, okay, well, 123 is still greater than 67.
52
Let's take everything to the right of 67,
53
and see if it's in there.
54
In this case, I'm down to one item and I found my thing.
55
So, if what you're looking for is bigger,
56
you move onto the second half of the portion of the array
57
you're currently looking at.
58
And if it's smaller,
59
you would move to the first half to the left.
60
And you just keep on splitting that as you go,
61
very much in the same spirit as a binary search tree.
62
All right.
63
So that's the algorithm,
64
and this is just like a binary search tree,
65
a log(n) operation.

--- Sort Algorithms ---

1
Continuing with our brief review
2
of computer science stuff,
3
let's talk about sorting algorithms next.

5

11
Like which one would you choose?
12

13
There's various kinds of sorting algorithms out there
14

15
or some sort of data structure.
16
When it's called Insertion sort,
17

18
and try to find the right spot to insert your new item in,
19
in a sorted manner.
20

21

22
So it's okay for small lists
23

24

But it's simple.
26
It has that going for it.
27
Merge Sort will be N log N.
28
So, because insertion sort has this kind
29
of horrible worst case of N squared,
30
Merge Sort is kind of in the middle there.
31
So yeh, it's not as good as N,
32
but N log N is a lot better than N squared.
33
So the advantage of Merge Sort is that it scales well
34
to very large lists.
35
And again, if you're being asked
36

37
you're probably being asked to design something
38
with massive amounts of data,
39
and Merge Sort is usually gonna be the right choice
40
over an Insertion Sort.
41
But there are other algorithms too.
42
Quicksort also technically, is O of N log N.

Unless you hit the worst case scenario of N squared,

45

47

49

We also have Bubble sort,
51

53
probably not the one you want.
54
And there are many other sorting algorithms
55
out there as well, but I think the takeaway here
56
is that if you have a massive amount of data,
57
Merge Sort is not a bad choice, Quicksort works,
58
but you know, a little bit harder to distribute that one.
59
So don't say you want to use Bubble sort,
60
that's a bad idea.
61
Insertion sort, not the best answer either.

--- Information Retrieval ---

It also appears in document ID 36 at position 1,235,
59
and also in document 432 again at position 55.
60
And similarly,
61
dinosaur a might map to documents 22, 724 and 552,
62
showing up in two different places in document 22.
63
We could then consolidate that down.
64
Why am I talking about palm trees and dinosaurs?
65
Well,
66
I happen to have some toy ones on my desk right now.
67
So, there you go.
68
So next we need to generate an inverted index
69
that maps keywords to documents.
70
So I know if I'm searching for a given keyword,
71
where do I go to find it?
72
So that's what we're talking about
73
in this second table here.
74
This is our inverted index
75
that's mapping keywords to specific document IDs.
76
And those are in some sort of an order
77
that represents relevance.
78
So in a very simple example here,
79
maybe we're ranking those documents
80
just based on how often that keyword appeared and where.
81
So in this example here,
82
we know that the word palm tree, or keyword palm tree,
83
actually appeared twice in document ID 432.
84
So we're gonna list that as the first choice,
85
with document ID 36 as a second choice
86
where it only appeared once.
87
Similarly, for the keyword dinosaur,
88
we know it actually appeared twice in document 22.
89
So we'll list that as our first result
90
and maybe 552 comes next
91
because it appeared earlier in that document
92
than it is in document 724.
93
So, that's basically the idea between a forward index
94
and an inverted index.
95
We generate this inverted index
96
that maps keywords to where that keyword appears
97
in different documents,
98
and maybe some other information that we can use
99
to determine the relevance.
100
And finally, we rank those document IDs
101
so that we can very quickly go from a keyword
102
to a ranked list of document IDs
103
that are relevant to that keyword somehow.
104
So another way of dealing with relevance
105
and kind of figuring out how to rank these things
106
is something called TF-IDF.
107
That stands for,
108
Term Frequency and Inverse Document Frequency.
109
Sounds really complicated, but it's a pretty simple concept.
110
It's another way of finding out what terms are most relevant
111
for a given document.
112
So you can't just look solely at quantity, right?
113
So if you did that,
114
the words like the, and a, and and would show up
115
as the most relevant terms for a given document,
116
and we know that's not the case.
117
So, TF-IDF is a pretty old way of dealing with that problem.
118
And you don't really see it in use too much these days,
119
but it is a fundamental thing.
120
It really is one of the oldest
121
and most basic search algorithms out there.
122
It still works pretty well, but you know,
123
these days more modern techniques tend to be used instead.
124
How does it work?
125
Okay, so term frequency is exactly what it sounds like.
126
It measures how often a word occurs in a document.
127
So a word that occurs really frequently
128
is probably important to that document's meaning.
129
Again, unless you're dealing with really common words,
130
like a, and and, and the, right?
131
But that stuff aside,
132
if I'm talking about dinosaurs a lot in a document,
133
the word dinosaur keeps appearing,
134
that's probably a relevant keyword for this document.
135
So the term frequency for dinosaur would be high
136
in that case.
137
Document frequency, however,
138
is how often a word occurs
139
across the entire set of documents.
140
So, let's say we're looking across all of Wikipedia
141
or across the entire web.
142
This tells us about those common words
143
that really aren't particular to a given document at all,
144
like a, and the, and and, and all that stuff.
145
So by looking at the relationship between these two things,
146
we get a measure of relevance.
147
So we take the term frequency,
148
how often a keyword appears within a given document.
149
And the document frequency,
150
how often that keyword appears across all the documents.
151
We arrive at this metric
152
that tells us how special this term is to this document.
153
You know, a high TF-IDF score
154
would mean that this keyword is...
155
It appears a lot within this particular document,
156
but it does not appear a lot across all the documents.
157
So it seems to be especially relevant to this document
158
that we're looking at.
159
Now, term frequency divided by document frequency
160
is mathematically the same thing as term frequency,
161
times the inverse of the document frequency.
162
That's why we call it TF-IDF.
163
All right, so it just gives you a measure
164
of how important and unique a given term is
165
within a given document.
166
It works all right.
167
So applying that to search,
168
a very simple search algorithm
169
might be to compute a TF-IDF for every word in a corpus.
170
You know, the entire set of webpages
171
or everything in your encyclopedia.
172
And then for a given search term,
173
sort those documents by their TF-IDF scores for that word
174
and display the results.
175
So that's one way of doing search.
176
Now, note that computing something like document frequency
177
can be intractable
178
if we're talking about a truly massive dataset, right?
179
If I want to count up how many times the word a appears
180
across the entire internet, that's not gonna happen.
181
And that's why you don't really see this
182
too often these days in practice
183
for truly massive search problems.
184
Speaking of truly massive search problems,
185
let's touch upon PageRank.
186
Kind of Google's original trick for searching the web.
187
And it was inspired by citations in academic papers.
188
So, this came out of Stanford, right? Originally.
189
And they lived in academia,
190
where the value of a given paper
191
was basically how many other papers
192
cited it as a reference later on.
193
So they applied the same idea to the web.
194
You know, if a given webpage is linked to a lot
195
by other pages, maybe it's important,
196
maybe it deserves to be ranked higher.
197
So, instead of relying entirely on the contents of the page,
198
like we're doing on the inverted index there,
199
where we keep track of how often a term appears
200
or things like TF-IDF,
201
they're also gonna be looking at the pages backlinks,
202
what is linking into this page,
203
and also the anchor text associated with those links.
204
Those might be also additional keywords
205
associated with that page.
206
So the high level idea here
207
is that a page with a lot of inbound links
208
means that it might be more useful.
209
And the anchor text on those links,
210
the actual text associated with that incoming link,
211
that backlink is treated like an additional keyword
212
for that page, all right?
213
And for any given backlink,
214
it is in turn weighted
215
by how many other links are on the page
216
that it's coming from.
217
So this kind of protects you from the link farms
218
that just link off
219
to all sorts of different things willy nilly.
220
That will penalize that sort of a thing.
221
So if you have a page
222
that just has a handful of external links,
223
those will count with more weight
224
in the PageRank algorithm.
225
We also have a damping factor d in the equation here,
226
and that just means
227
that we're not gonna follow links forever.
228
As we search further and further down
229
through the tree of links,
230
we're gonna assign less and less weight
231
as we work our way through.
232
So the original PageRank algorithm
233
is here on the right here
234
that Google originally used and published,
235
where the PageRank of a given page
236
is going to be one minus the damping factor d,
237
plus the damping factor,
238
times the page where a backlink came from
239
for that first backlink
240
over how many backlinks were on that page,
241
plus the PageRank of the next backlink we have
242
over the number of links from that backlinks page,
243
so on and so forth.
244
However, these days Google has moved
245
way past things like PageRank and TF-IDF.
246
These days, deep learning tends to be the weapon of choice
247
for doing ranking at least, in search applications.
248
So, good to know about how they work
249
and to be able to speak to it.
250
But you need to know that in modern times,
251
PageRank and TF-IDF aren't actually used a lot.
252
So that's our discussion about search
253
and information retrieval in a very brief nutshell.
254
But you know,
255
these are all additional tools to have in your tool chest
256
when you're solving a given design problem.
257
And we'll come back to this
258
in some of our mock interviews later on.

--- Message Queues ---

1
Real quickly, let's talk about message cues as well.
2
It's also potentially a useful tool in your tool chest for
3
solving a system design problem.
4
So message queues are a way to deal with some specific
5
scaling problems.
6
The idea here is that it's a mechanism for decoupling
7
producers and consumers of data.
8
So it's sort of a buffer we can use to make sure that things
9
don't get backed up in a larger system.
10
So with a message queue in place,
11
if your consumers of data get backed up a little bit,
12
you don't have quite enough capacity there, it's okay.
13
You're not going to lose data.
14
It might sit in that message queue for a while,
15
and it might take a little bit longer to process,
16
but at least you're not going to lose it, right?
17
It's going to be somewhere safe,
18
waiting to be processed when you can get to it.
19
An example of an implementation is the Amazon SQS service,
20
or Simple Queue Service.
21
If you want to just use an off the shelf, you know,
22
cloud based solution,
23
SQS is an example of a single-consumer message queue.
24
So in SQS, there is only one consumer associated
25
with a given publisher of data.
26
However, there's also the idea
27
of pub/sub or published subscribe,
28
where you can actually have messages published
29
by your servers and multiple consumers
30
can then get that data back out from it,
31
through that message queue.
32
So what's an example of this?
33
You know, let's say I have a bunch
34
of web servers out there that are
35
generating orders or something, right?
36
And those orders need to get set to sent
37
to some other system for further processing
38
and fraud detection and validation.
39
What happens if my systems that do fraud detection get
40
backed up, right?
41
Do I just throw those orders away?
42
No, that would be bad.
43
You know, I don't want to leave money lying on the table or
44
give my customer a bad experience
45
when they're trying to give me money.
46
Instead, I'd have some sort of a message queue
47
in between these two
48
systems so that if the consumers,
49
I need to process that data,
50
do get backed up,
51
it'll sit in the queue until
52
it can actually get caught up again.
53
And again, there are single consumer systems like SQS,
54
and then there are pubs subsystems where you can have
55
multiple consumers for a given publisher that subscribed to
56
a given message queue.
57
Now this is a little bit different from streaming data.
58
So when we talk about streaming data,
59
like in the context of Kafka
60
or Spark Streaming or things like that,
61
those are generally in the context
62
of real time massive data that just needs
63
to be routed from one
64
system quickly to another.
65
Technically systems like Kafka
66
and Spark Streaming can be used as a message queue,
67
but message queues tend to be a little bit more
68
single-threaded if you will.
69
Okay, so it's meant more as a buffer
70
between two systems and not as
71
mechanism for piping torrents of data
72
between them necessarily.
73
And speaking of Apache Spark,
74
let's talk about that next.

--- Apache Spark ---

1
So let's spend a little bit of time
2

3

10
in the Hadoop ecosystem?
11
Well, when you're running a Hadoop cluster,
12
at its lowest level, we have the HDFS file system
13

14
That's distributing the storage of your data
15
across the cluster.

On top of that, in the world of Hadoop 2 at least,

20

21

22
Now, sitting on top of that, way back in the day,
23

24
That was the old Google technology
25

26
And the idea of MapReduce was pretty simple.
27
Basically, you'd have a mapping process
28
that would extract the data that you need
29
from the raw data being fed into it
30
on that underlying storage, and then a reduce stage
31

32
into whatever way you need it,
33
and arrive at the aggregate results that you're after,
34
which worked great,
35

36

37
And oftentimes, there were more efficient ways of doing it
38
that you might not think about easily.
39
And you were pretty limited
40

41
Like, they didn't give you a whole lot of flexibility.
42
So that's when Spark came into play,
43
and Apache Spark is basically a replacement for MapReduce,
44
and it's pretty much better in every way these days.
45
So with Spark, you can write your scripts in Java, Scala,
46
Python, or R.
47
Scala and Python tend to be
48

49
And you can tell it to do
50

51

52

53
across your entire cluster.
54
So instead of trying to like,
55
thinking about how do I break this down
56

57
In fact, these days,
58
people usually just give it SQL commands
59
and Spark will go off and figure out, okay,
60
how can I break this SQL command into chunks
61
that I can process in parallel
62
and distribute across my entire cluster efficiently
63
and bring those results back together for a final answer?
64
And it's also a lot faster than MapReduce
65
because it relies heavily on in-memory caching.
66
So it's trying to minimize disk use as much as possible.
67
And it's also creating what we call
68
a directed acyclic graph, or a DAG,

70
and how it arrives at a final answer.
71
It also supports code reuse across batch processing,
72
like we talked about.

That's probably the most common use case of Spark
74
where you have just a giant pile of data.

And every so often, you need to boil that down

or transform that data in some way
78
to store it someplace more permanent.
79
Maybe you just need to like suck out some raw log data,
80

and store the final results in a database somewhere.
82
You can use Spark for interactive queries.
83
There is a interactive interface for Spark SQL.

Like I said, SQL has sort of become

just like you would to a normal relational database

and use that for near-real-time,

or actually, using Spark Streaming these days,
96
actual real-time analytics of data as it's coming in.

It's also widely used these days for machine learning.
99
So another big advantage of Spark compared to MapReduce

So there's something called MLLib that has a wide variety

Let's talk a little bit more about Spark Streaming,

So that's another common use of Spark.

How does Spark itself work?
154
Well, this itself is a system design problem, right?
155
So at a high level, Spark applications are run
156

It actually has its own built-in

Somewhere you have a script that says,

That driver program runs within

All right, make sense?

or large language models

First of all, we can think of AI
for the purpose of this course

you know, a large language model

So, we're not going to get into how large language models

So, we're gonna think of that
as basically a black box of AI, right?
Now, a little bit of a disclaimer here.
AI does not mean generative AI.
It does not just, it's not limited to large language models
or image generation.
AI is a much broader term,
and we're not gonna talk about the broader term of AI.

because, well, that's what's hot these days, right?
But AI also encompasses all of machine learning.
It encompasses all of the expert systems,
all the classical machine learning algorithms out there,
like, you know, regression, XGBoost, all that good stuff.
That's also AI.
But we're just talking about generative AI here.
Now, what we call these underlying models

So, that's what we call these sort of,
general purpose engines
that are massive things that were trained on

They don't necessarily
have data specific to your application
or to your organization,

and they've also been tuned to basically, you know,
behave the way that we want them to behave.
You know, actually have a little bit of
additional reinforcement learning
on top of the underlying statistical model
that generates the text, if you will.
Now we communicate through these with APIs.
So, all of these models will have APIs,
so you can call from your larger system.
So you don't have to just, you know,
go to a website and type in questions and get answers back.
The way it works is that you have an API of some sort.
So for example, with OpenAI, the makers of ChatGPT,
they offer a chat completions API,
which is just a way of sending text back and forth
between you and the model.
So, for example, I might have an overarching system prompt
that kind of guides the entire conversation,
and I might say, okay, always talk like a pirate, right?
So I want you to always gimme back an answer
in the style of talking like a pirate.
That's my system prompt.
It's gonna apply to everything that I do going forward
with that model.
And then I have individual prompts,
like, what is the meaning of life?
So, I might combine those together
into a chat completions API call to OpenAI.
It will go off into that black box
and that giant throbbing brain in the cloud
and come back with an output.
That might be,
"Arr! The answer be 42",
and that would be the output that I get back.

So, the actual code that you might look at

"Arr! The answer be 42", or whatever it might be, right?
So, incorporating AI into a larger system

But, just know that there is an API for fine tuning.
The idea is that you can build upon these models,
and continue their training with your own proprietary data.
So, one way of doing that
would be to feed it a series of prompts
and optimal responses.
Or you know, maybe a data store of,
given this input, this is the output I would expect, right?
So, by feeding it that
you're basically extending the training of that model
and starting to customize it for your own purposes.
That's what we call fine tuning.
Again, not gonna get into the details for this course,
'cause that's not really a system design thing.
There might be also be an API for embeddings.
So, under the hood, when you give it a prompt,
the first thing it's gonna do,
or one of the first things it's going to do,
is convert that into basically a bunch of vectors
that represent the meanings of the words that you're saying.
Those are what we call embedding vectors.
And these can be useful,
because if you compare embedding vectors
for different terms or sentences,
or chunks of text or whatever it might be,
you can get some measure of
how similar they are semantically.
It basically encompasses the meaning of those words,
if you will.
And we'll see very shortly how we might build upon that
to build a architecture
called retrieval augmented generation.
So, stay tuned, that's embeddings.
Now, another way of basically extending these systems
to work with your own custom proprietary data
is to just jam it into the context, right?
So these modern large language models
have large context windows.
That means that we can
feed it a whole lot of information at once,
and tell it to do something with it.
So we don't just have to type in a very short prompt,
we can include a bunch of contextual information
with that prompt if we want to.
So, this example on the right here, I'm saying,

and just jam that into the prompt.
We're calling that context to make it sound fancy,
but, really we're just making
a really, really, really long prompt,
that contains all the information that the LLM needs
to construct a valid response,
incorporating that data into the response.
So, we just take this huge blob of data,
and we shove it into the chat completions API,
and it comes back and says, great, here we go.
Here's a quiz question, what does LLM stand for?
Large language model.
And that's just an example.
So, a lot of people are saying
that maybe more complicated architectures
are becoming obsolete,
because you can just shove everything you need
into that context.
However, this does come with a caveat.
You know, these companies have kind of an ulterior motive
of pushing you in that direction,
because they tend to charge you by the token.
So, basically by the word
that you're feeding into it as input,
and getting back as output.
So, if you're gonna be putting that entire transcript
along with every single prompt that you feed to the system,
that's gonna add up fast,
you know, you don't necessarily wanna be doing that.
It might be better to,
somehow cache that information somewhere.
By the way, just as an aside,
that context is also how we maintain,
sort of the flow of conversation
throughout a conversation with an AI.
So, we just keep appending new questions and answers,
to that prompt as it goes in.
So, under the hood,
we're just gonna help with a giant prompt
that contains all the history of that chat,
including the latest question that you're asking.
So, it kind of cheats a little bit there.
But basically the idea here is that,
you can just jam a bunch of proprietary information
into that prompt and say,
you know, use this as part of your response, and that works.
You know, you can give it the entire document
that you want to ask about,
say, summarize this huge document, right?
Or give me an answer to this question
based on this entire data store
of sample questions and answers, whatever it might be.
However, this can get expensive again,
because you're being charged by the token.
So, if you end up replicating
that massive context information with every single prompt,
that's a real waste of resources.
So, maybe there's a better way of doing it.

--- Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) ---

-: So let's do some actual system design
around generative AI.
And one common architecture
is retrieval augmented generation or RAG for short.
And the idea is pretty straightforward.
You can think of it as basically an open book exam
for your large language model.
So the idea is you have a prompt
in this example,
maybe what did the president say in his speech yesterday?
And your RAG architecture there will then go off
to some external data store that you've built up
to kind of look up the answer.
You know, cheat to win.
So maybe I have some external database
that's a up-to-date database
of every presidential speech
that's updated every night somehow, right?
And I can query that database
and say, okay, given this prompt,
what did the president say in his speech yesterday?
Find the most relevant entry in that data store
of speeches that are associated with that query.
And if we're smart about how we do that query,
we can basically find the answer at that stage
before we've even talked to our large language model
and inject that answer into part of the prompt
as part of the context that we send in with it.
So again, we're cheating to win here.
Basically we're saying,
I'm gonna look up in this external database,
maybe it's a vector store,
we'll talk about what that means in a second
to get the answer ahead of time
before I actually feed that into my large language model.
So when I get that information
that I've kind of gone off
and gotten externally just through traditional search,
I can inject that as part of the prompt,
feed that to my large language model,
and then get back the answer.
So it might look like this on the right here.
Basically I'm saying, here's my query.
I'm looking up some relevant contextual information
from an external data store.
I'm just gonna concatenate that to the query
and say, as part of the prompt,
what did the president say in his speech yesterday?
Consider the text of the following
news article in your response.
And maybe there I have the text of a news article
that came out about that speech.
And at that point,
I'm just gonna feed that into my large language model.
And its job is just to extract the relevant information
from that context and basically massage the answer
into something more conversational that makes more sense.
So at this point, we're not really using the AI
as a source of answers.
We're just using that AI
to formulate the response in natural language.
Now this has some advantages, right?
So first of all, we don't have to include
every speech ever as part of the prompt.
You know, we can just sort of pick
and choose the most relevant bits of information
that we have and that can save on the number of tokens
that we're sending in, which can save money.
Incorporating that data that you have externally
can also reduce the hallucinations
or the the tendencies
that these systems have of making stuff up.
So by giving it relevant information,
it doesn't have to make stuff up because it doesn't know.
And unfortunately, large language models
have a tendency to do that.
Also, this is a very easy way
to include new and fresh data.
So this particular example is relevant
because these speeches might be changing every day, right?
And if I just update that database
every night with new information, I'm done.
I don't have to go and retrain my model from scratch.
I don't have to fine-tune it.
This is a very inexpensive way
of making sure I have up-to-date data
and making it very easy to change
and modify that data going forward
without having to modify this massive model under the hood.
So let's go into a little bit more depth with this.
So let's go into the example of a system
that can answer jeopardy questions
on the TV game show Jeopardy.
So maybe I start with a query that's like in 1986,
Mexico scored as the first country
to host this international sports competition twice.
So at that point,
I can go into what we call a retriever model.
Now the first thing I'm gonna do is encode that query.
So basically I'm gonna say, okay,
I'm gonna boil that prompt down into some vector
that represents its underlying meaning.
And now I'm gonna query this database
of all the jeopardy questions I've ever seen before
and find the vector of those questions
that most closely matches the vector of that prompt.
And that might give me as many documents as I want.
So maybe I say, okay, give me back the top four results
that are most relevant to that prompt.
So I'm gonna go off to my database
of every jeopardy question ever.
Find the four answers that are basically the most relevant
to that prompt in 1986, Mexico scored, blah, blah, blah.
I will then incorporate that into the actual prompt.
So I'll say, please find the jeopardy answer
to this following question.
Include these following potential answers in your response,
and we'll go off
and send that off to the LLM
that's part of the generator phase.
And it might come back with a very simple answer
of what is the World Cup based on that information.
So the LLM there again,
is sort of distilling the information that you've given it,
but you're providing it
with that extra dose of relevant context
to kind of help it get to where it needs to go.
So that is RAG in a nutshell.
Basically, we're just handing the generator
the large language model
that you're querying under the hood,
the potential answers from an external database,
and letting it use that to kind of guide its response
and make it more likely to get the right answer.
So when we talk about how we actually do that lookup,
how do we find the relevant bits of information
to that prompt?
Well, we have to understand
what embedding vectors are to do that.
So an embedding is just a giant vector.
It might be hundreds or even thousands of dimensions
associated with your data, okay?
So you can think of it
as this point in multidimensional space
that represents the semantics
of what you're trying to say.
Or you could think of it as sort of the meaning
of what you're trying to say.
So the idea is that in this multidimensional space,
embedding vectors that are close to each other
are gonna be more close to each other in meaning.
So in this silly example on the right
I have the words potato and rhubarb.
Those might have embedding vectors
that are very close to each other.
In this example,
we're just boiling it down to two dimensions.
And those might represent, you know, root vegetables
or whatever it's right.
And then I might have some other embedding vectors
that represent other phrases
like The starship Enterprise or Battlestar Galactica
or the Millennium Falcon.
Obviously these are science fiction starships,
and those might also be very close
to each other in this embedding space.
Now how do we create these embedding vectors?
Well, that is also a machine learning problem.
That's also an AI problem.
So that's a whole other can of worms,
but it's basically this entirely separate
deep neural network that has been trained
on how to actually assign meanings
to bits of text, if you will.
So once we have that, we can do stuff with it.
We can actually do vector queries to see, okay,
are these two things similar to each other?
And open AI and many other models as well
offer an API to create these at scale
very cheaply, by the way.
So you don't really have to worry about
how do I create these embedding vectors.
You probably have access to a very inexpensive API
to do this for you at scale.
So you just say, here's a chunk of text,
give me back an embedding vector.
It says, okay, here you go.
And you can do that in a batch fashion too,
and do a whole lot at once.
So that makes it very cheap
and very easy to compute these things.
Once you have those embeddings,
you might wanna store them in a vector database, right?
So this is just storing your data
alongside their computing embedding vector.
So think back to that example of this database
of presidential speeches.
Maybe I'm storing the text of that speech
along with its vector.
Its embedding vector representation.
This will allow me to very quickly look up speeches
that are semantically similar
to some other bit of text,
like the prompt that I'm trying to query.
Now again, this is just leveraging
the embeddings that you already have.
You know, we have to create these embeddings anyway
for a large language model to work.
So you're just taking advantage of a system
that's already there here.
So with embeddings in the mix,
here's what retrieval would actually look like
in a RAG system.
Basically, you start by computing an embedding vector
for the thing you want to search for
for the prompt, you know?
So if I'm saying,
what did the president say in his speech last night?
I have an embedding vector that can represent that question.
And now I'm gonna query my vector database
for the top items that are closest to that vector.
This is what we call sometimes semantic search
or more accurately vector search.
And it will get me back the top
and most similar things to that prompt.
So I'm gonna say, hey,
here's an embedding vector of what I'm looking for.
Give me back the top documents
that are closest in meaning to that query.
And that's what comes back from my context, right?
So what is the vector database?
Well, there are very many ways of doing it.
So because generative AI is so popular now,
there's a lot of databases out there
that offer this capability.
Elasticsearch has tacked that on as well.
There's a lot of SQL databases that let you do this too.
Neptune on the AWS side, Redis can do this,
MongoDB can do this, Cassandra can do this.
Pretty much every database out there
can do embedding searches these times,
or vector searches, if you will.
There are also purpose built
vector databases out there as well.
Like Pinecone is one, Weaviate, Chroma, Marqo,
this whole other list.
I'm sure there's more by now,
but there's a lot of them out there
because this is sort of an integral piece
of a lot of generative AI architectures.
So that's the vector store.
Again, we take the prompt,
we compute a vector, an embedding vector for it.
We look up the closest embedding vectors
to bits of text in our database,
include that in our prompt,
shove it into our LLM,
and hopefully get back a relevant response.
That's RAG in a nutshell.
Let's take another look at an example here.
So let's say I built an AI system
that can answer questions about "Star Trek"
because, well, I like "Star Trek."
So let's say the prompt is data.
Tell me about your daughter Lal.
And if you aren't familiar with "Star Trek,"
data is the Android on the show
and he made an Android daughter once named Lal.
So first thing I'm gonna do,
compute an embedding vector there.
So that's gonna involve an API call,
probably to open AI or something else that says,
here's a chunk of text,
give me back an embedding vector.
So that's one piece of the architecture right there.
Once I have that, I can query my vector database
of everything data has ever said potentially, right?
So I'm gonna say, okay, here's an embedding vector
that talks about data and his daughter Lal.
Let's do a vector query against this vector database
of everything data ever said.
And that will probably give me back a set of documents
where data's talking about his daughter Lal.
And I can incorporate that information into my prompt.
So I get back this set of end similar lines
from that data store based on that vector query
of those embedding vectors, okay?
And maybe my prompt will look something like this.
You are commander data from "Star Trek."
How might data respond to the question,
data tell me about your daughter Lal,
taking into account the following related lines from data.
And then I just append those similar lines,
those relevant lines, those semantically similar lines
that came back from my vector store.
And then I shoved that into my large language model
again using an API call
and I get back from that API to the large language model,
that black box, that giant throbbing brain in the cloud
that contains hopefully a good answer based on that data
that I gave it based on that extra context
that came from my vector store.
So we take our prompt, we embed that into the query
that goes to our LLM along with the relevant data
that we looked up from our vector store,
and that should enable us to get back a better answer.
That's RAG, retrieval augmented generation in a nutshell.

--- Agentic AI, and Integrating AI into Larger Systems ---

-: Another architecture in generative AI
is what we call LLM agents or agentic AI.
These terms are used kind of interchangeably these days.
The idea is that we're giving external tools
to your large language model.
So when we talked about APIs, so large language models,
basically that chat completions API call
also has the ability to provide external functions
and tools as part of that query to the engine.
So along with your prompt, you can say, by the way,
if you see a question about this specific kind of thing,
you can access this external system
and go look up the answer through some other architecture
that's outside of this world entirely, potentially.
So for example, maybe I might have a system that says,
hey, if this person actually asks about what the weather is,
see if you can find out what city
and what day they're talking about
and go out and talk to this weather API
that I have for some external weather service
and incorporate that into the response.
That's a very simple example of an agent.
So the LLM itself is given discretion
on which tools to use for which purpose.
So maybe I have multiple tools.
Maybe there's this weather API,
maybe there's this external vector store lookup
for like I would be using for a RAG system, right?
It could be anything.
Maybe it's some system that controls my web browser.
Who knows, right? It can be a bunch of different things.
You just describe in plaintext to it
when to use those tools
and what the interface is to those tools
that it needs to fill out.
It's pretty cool, pretty magic.
So the agent,
conceptually, let's back up a little bit.
So when people started inventing LLM agents,
they kind of thought about it in these abstract terms.
So an agent has a couple of different things.
A memory, an ability to plan how to answer a request,
and the tools that it can use
to process that request, right?
So in practice,
the memory is just gonna be the chat history.
It's like the context, right?
And it's also any external data storage
that I might be giving the system access to.
So that's its memory if you will.
The planning module is just the guidance that I'm giving it
on how to break down a question into sub-questions
that these tools might be able to help with.
So the planning module is just saying,
hey, if someone's asking about the weather
as part of this query,
maybe you wanna ship that off
to this weather service, right, for example.
It's just giving the system some information
about what kind of tools it has for what kinds of problems,
and allowing it to figure out
which systems it needs to talk to to fulfill this prompt.
So generally speaking, you just say in plain English,
you know, this is the external system that you wanna use.
This is the situation where you have to use it,
and here's some information about the API
to actually call that
and the information that I need to pass in,
the parameters that I need to come in and get back from it.
Again, I'm not gonna get into the details
because we're focusing on the system design part of it here.
So from a system design standpoint,
at a more practical level, this is what it might look like.
So I have a user request coming in that's like,
tell me what the weather is in Anchorage, Alaska tomorrow.
And that goes into my age agentic framework, if you will.
And that agentic framework
will have multiple tools associated with it.
Maybe some of that's, you know, a retriever
for some external database,
but one of them might be, here's a weather service.
So I have a prompt under the hood that tells the LLM,
hey, if you're asking about the weather,
make sure you get the specific city
and location and whatnot.
Anything I need to fulfill that request.
Here's the actual parameters that I need to pass in.
And that will just give it to a bit of Python code
within your application that then goes off
and talks to that external service, right?
So basically I just have a Python script
that has functions that can talk to external data sources.
The LLM is given some guidance through its prompts
as when to call back to those functions.
So sometimes if you do a chat completions API call,
what you get back won't be the final answer.
It'll say, hey, can you call this function for me instead
to kinda like give me some more information that I need.
And you say, okay, I'll go call that for you.
Here's the answer, try again.
And then you finally get back the final answer
in this system.
So these tools can do anything.
They can be anything, they can be outside information,
retrieving data internally, Python modules that do anything,
things they call services.
The sky's the limit, you know.
Your imagination can run wild here.
So this is kind of giving your AI access to the real world.
What could go wrong, right?
One little sub-note here or footnote here.
There is something called Model Context Protocol
or MCP for short that was developed by Anthropic
and it is being pretty widely adopted these days.
It's basically a standard for connecting tools to agents.
So it's saying, okay,
here's just this basically general standard
of how I describe what these tools can do,
how do I describe what their expected
inputs and outputs are?
It's actually a lot simpler than it sounds,
but it might be relevant to a system design problem
where you're saying, oh, well,
how do I actually connect to these tools?
You can say, well, I use MCP,
so, you know, potential answer
to an interview question there.
So that's agentic AI. Now it's pretty simple, right?
So basically you just have an AI system,
you tell it that it can use these external tools.
You write the code that actually accesses those tools
and gets the answers back,
incorporate that into the final response.
That's agentic AI. Not hard, right?
Now, keep in mind too that these AI systems
can be part of a larger system.
So generally speaking, you're not gonna be having
your end users talking directly to OpenAI,
like through your endpoint with your credentials.
That could get pretty scary from a cost
and security standpoint, right?
So typically speaking,
these AI subsystems are gonna be part of a larger system.
So I can take all the architecture
that we've talked about previously in this course,
you know, maybe I have a suite
of external application clients there,
talk them through the cloud
and they go to a load balancer
to a bunch of application servers.
Those are actually running my script
that actually processes these requests.
Maybe even cache it, you know,
because these API calls to AI can be expensive.
If I'm getting a lot of the same questions
over and over and over again,
maybe I wanna cache those responses
and not regenerate them every time, right?
So maybe there's a caching layer involved there as well.
And maybe underneath there,
there's our agentic framework that we just talked about.
It's just another layer in this larger system, right?
So it's just another piece of the puzzle, folks.
You know, the other stuff you've learned
about distributed computing and scaling and caching,
it all still applies here.
It's just built around what is essentially another service,
a very intelligent service, if you will.
And again, you know, as we talk to that agentic system,
those tools themselves might be their own individual systems
that we can talk about the design of as well, right?
So if that's involving talking to a vector store,
maybe I wanna talk about how I scale that out, you know.
And if it is just a cloud service,
maybe I don't have to worry about it.
But if it's Elasticsearch, maybe I talk about having,
you know, a suite of Elasticsearch domains
or whatever it is.
So there's a lot of system design stuff
to talk about in these systems.
Again, not getting into the details of how AI works here.
If you want more depth on AI itself,
well that's a different course,
but again, it's just a piece of these larger systems.
And the key takeaways here
are Retrieval Augmented Generation
because that is a very system architecturally kind of thing.
And also the agentic frameworks or AI agents,
which is another way of solving
a very similar problem
in a more robust or extensible manner, if you will.
So from a system design standpoint, that's pretty much it.
That's generative AI as part of your larger systems.

they're trying to find a video and play it back,

you know, in a reliable manner.
209
They don't want to see any buffering, right.
210
And which use cases do you need to concern yourself with?
211
So again, don't paint yourself into a corner
212
where you're trying to design all of YouTube in 20 minutes
213
or all of Google or all of Amazon.
214
Make sure you define upfront what piece of it
215
they really want you to do.
216
So your first task in any system design interview,
217
I'm just going to keep hammering this home,
218
clarify the requirements
219
of what you're being asked to design, all right.
220
And do that in terms
221
of the customer experience whenever possible.
222
Your interviewer is really trying to see
223
that you can think about problems
224
from a business perspective
225
and not just a technical perspective.
226
So if you start with the customer experience
227
and focus on that,
228
that shows me that you can think about this like a business.
229
You know, you're not just thinking about the technology.
230
You're thinking about how this technology
231
will provide an experience to this company's customers
232
that can make them money down the road, right.
233
That's what we're all here for in a company.
234
It's not just about, you know,
235
being cool and applying cool technology.
236
It's about finding solutions
237
for delivering new customer experiences
238
that customers find compelling.

--- Defining Requirements ---

1
So once I've defined the customer experience
2
I'm trying to deliver,
3
what do we ask about next?
4
Well, probably we're gonna wanna
5
define the scaling requirements of the system
6
I'm being asked to deliver.
7
Right? So nail down what the scale
8
of the system is really.
9
Are we talking about hundreds of users or millions of users?
10
That's gonna make a big difference
11
as to what sort of technologies I invoke.
12
And that will inform you on the need
13
for horizontal partitioning.
14
So if the scale is massive,
15
then we know we need some sort
16
of horizontally scaled solution.
17
We can't just throw one giant database at it
18
or one giant web server.
19
We need some sort of distributed system.
20
And if it's truly massive, you know,
21
maybe we need to rely on cloud storage solutions like S3,
22
that where somebody else is managing all that cost
23
and complexity for us.
24
Also understand how often the users are coming.
25
What kind of transaction rates are we talking about here
26
that we need to support?
27
Again, if it's large rates of transactions
28
and you're gonna need a large number of servers
29
at the front end to manage those transactions.
30
And maybe you need to think about how those transactions
31
are distributed across different data centers
32
and across the world.
33
Also define the scale of the data, okay.
34
So you know, that data has to come from somewhere.
35
It probably needs to be processed in some way.
36
Are we talking about hundreds of videos
37
or millions of videos, billions of videos.
38
If so, if it's a really big number,
39
then we need some sort of distributed system
40

41
And you know, for YouTube, obviously,
42

43
millions of users, millions of videos, maybe even billions,

I think these days.
45
And you're gonna need to employ every trick in the book
46

and your data storage and your data processing.

49

50
If you're being asked to design some internal tool,
51
that's only be being used by, you know,
52

Maybe you don't need that level of complexity and expense.
54
You should always prefer

56
Generally speaking,
57
if you're being asked to do a system design
58

59
they're asking you to design something at massive scale.
60
More often than not,
61

62

you should think about it at least articulate that,

'Hey, this is massive scale.

In the off chance that you're being asked to design

You know, I don't want to have to maintain
74
all those services if I don't need to.
75

76

77
Why take on that complexity
78
where I can just have a database
79
and a backup database and a few web servers
80
in front of it and be done with it, right?
81
So just again, think out loud.
82
And if you're going in the wrong direction,
83
that gives the interviewer a chance to steer you
84
in the direction that he wants or she wants.
85
Also define your latency requirements.
86
How quickly do you need to return these values?
87
How fast is fast enough?
88
So that might tell you about
89
the need for caching and CDN usage, right?
90

91

But if your requirements there
95

Remember too, caching isn't just about latency and speed.
98

99

100

101

102

103
So caching is not just for
104

105

106
And if you can, try to express this in the language of SLAs,
107
the percentiles we talked about.
108
So, you know, say we're shooting
109

121
do I need to cache their recommendations for videos?

--- Design Strategies ---

1
All right, I've said this before,
2
but I'm gonna say it again.
3
Think out loud during your interview.
4
Don't just clam up for 10 minutes
5
while you think about things.
6
For all you know, you only have 15 minutes for this question
7
and you don't want to spend 10 minutes of it
8
just sitting there in silence where your interviewer
9
has to just wonder what you're thinking.
10
Do not do that.
11
Think out loud,
12
use that time to clarify requirements,
13
define the constraints of what you need to build,
14

15

16
to meet those requirements.
17
Say what you're thinking.
18

19

20
to process the data down here

22

23
Just talk and think,
24
let the interviewer see how you're approaching this problem
25

26
And that's important because it gives the interviewer
27
not only a chance to see how you think,
28

29
and see how you respond to that guidance, right?
30
Part of what they're trying to evaluate here
31

32

Think out loud.
35

--- Web Crawler: System Design ---

1
Okay, let me start by thinking about it
2
from an algorithmic standpoint.
3
Basically webpages are vertices on a directed graph,
4
and the links between them
5
are the edges of the graph.
6
So fundamentally this is a graph traversal problem.
7
Right, so what kind of traversal
8
would you do here?
9
Well, the choices are breadth for a search
10
or depth for a search.
11
So let me think about that for a second.
12
Well, the number of links
13
on any one page are pretty finite,
14
so that would represent breadth,
15
but the depth of the internet is pretty much infinite.
16
So I think that makes BFS
17
the only real trackable solution here.
18
Okay, remind me how BFS works.
19
All right, so starting at some page,
20
you go through every link on the page
21
and kick off the processing of each link
22
to some other process in the name of scalability,
23
I would think.
24
And then each link on the child nodes are processed
25
working your way across the graph from left to right,
26
as opposed to DFS,
27
where we would follow one path all the way down to the end
28
and then back up and follow the other path
29
all the way down to the end
30
and so on and so forth.
31
The problem is that following any path
32
all the way to the end
33
would take pretty much forever on the internet.
34
So BFS is usually the way to go,
35
and this seems like no exception.
36
Okay, good.
37
Let's get to the hard part
38
and make this scale to billions of web pages.
39
All right, let me start with something simple
40
and high level, and then we can start refining it.
41
Makes sense.

--- Search Engine: System Design ---

1
Well, let me first start by thinking about
2
a high level algorithmic approach
3
to how to identify documents relative to a keyword.
4
The most basic search algorithm I know is TF-IDF
5
where we take the frequency of a term within a document
6
and divide it by the frequency of the term
7
over all of the documents
8
to get a measure of how "special" that term is
9
to that document.
10
Yep, can you think of any problems
11
that might present
12
in the context of searching the entire web?
13
Yeah, at least two come to mind.
14
One is that this is really easy to game;
15
so just load up a page with the keyword you want,
16
and make sure it's a relatively obscure keyword,
17
and you win.
18
So, maybe this could be part of the solution
19
but certainly something more sophisticated is needed.
20
The other problem is computing the denominator.
21
Computing document frequency across the entire Internet
22
seems like an intractable problem,
23
or at least one that would require so many resources
24
that we would should question if we really need it.
25
Right, so,
26
let's see what other ideas you can come up with.
27
Well, let me work backwards
28
from what we need to present to the user.
29
So ultimately, we need some sort of inverted index
30
that maps keywords to a list of relevant documents
31
sorted by some measure of relevance.
32
So, what might that measure be?
33
Well, we've all heard of "PageRank",
34
it's how Google started.
35
As I recall that's based in part on backlinks;
36
they were using links to a page
37
and the anchor text of those links
38
as measures of a site's popularity.
39
Coming from academia, they just saw backlinks
40
as the same thing as citations for research papers,
41
which is kind of how they measure success
42
in the academic world.
43
But I digress.
44
Let me just start writing down things
45
we might want to measure
46
and bake into whatever generates that inverted index.
47
So, backlinks is one.
48
What else can I think of?
49
Obviously the words actually on the page
50
should count for something,
51
but there may be other signals
52
that give us hints on how much to weight those terms.
53
Like what position within the document it's in;
54
if it's mentioned right at the beginning,
55
then it's probably relevant to the page.
56
Words that appear in bold or in a big font
57
might also be more important.
58
The title of the page is a big signal too;
59
words in the title should count for a lot.
60
I suppose that's something that could be gamed,
61
but if the title is what's presented
62
to user in the search results,
63
it kind of has to be relevant to the page
64
if the page owner expects
65
to get relevant user traffic in return.
66
The length of the document might also be a signal,
67
or it may be more generally
68
how often the term appears in the document.
69
Hey, that's term frequency;
70
maybe we can at least keep that part of it.
71
The idea is to make sure that we don't rank pages
72
that just have the keyword on it and nothing else.
73
I think there are meta-tags
74
people can insert into their HTML
75
to explicitly tell search engines
76
what keywords they want to target,
77
but that seems really prone to gaming,
78
so I'm not sure we'd really want to use that
79
in this day and age.
80
Just thinking out loud, these all look like features
81
that could ultimately be fed into a neural network
82
that learns how to rank documents
83
based on user interaction with search results.
84
That might play a role later on in the design
85
but first let's worry about extracting
86
and storing all of this data at scale.
87
OK, so we'll start with that repository of web pages
88
that came from our crawler.
89
Presumably this is compressed somehow,
90
so as not to take up ridiculous amounts of storage
91
or at least less ridiculous amounts of storage.
92
Now we need to build up an index
93
that maps keywords found in each page
94
to the document it was found in,
95
its position within the document,
96
and whether it's a header or a title,
97
and the other signals that we talked about a minute ago.
98
Basically, this is a big key/value object store
99
where the keys are keywords, or more compactly keyword ID's.
100
So, that can be distributed.
101
I think that's kind of what BigTable was made for at Google,
102
but any modern NoSQL database should do.
103
In the end, we want an inverted index
104
that maps keywords to a ranked list of documents,
105
so if we can keep that sorted by keyword
106
as we go somehow that will save us some trouble,
107
but we could do it later if we had to.
108
As for the indexer itself,
109
I think that's why Google invented MapReduce
110

--- Think Big and Be Nice ---

1
Another tip is to always think big,
2
if you're interviewing at a big technical company,
3
pretty much everything you do has to be a massive scale,
4
massive amounts of data, massive requirements,
5
so any system you design needs to hold up to petabytes
6
of data, thousands of transactions per second,
7
it's what this whole course has been about,
8

10

11

12
So thinking big,
13

14
or big transaction rates, it's thinking about
15

16
the customer experiences that you're trying to deliver.
17

18
how does the thing I build actually result in something
19
beneficial to the company?
20
Don't just think in terms of technology, okay.
21

22
it's how you apply that technology
23
to making customers happier that matters.
24

25

28

29
to work with, right?
